A rotation re-reads the key from disk and refuses one another node revoked, and key records are replaced atomically so concurrent writes cannot leave a partial record.
- **Let a surface name only its own API keys in an Identity** — A from_api_key Identity, identity injection or Transit Point identity now saves only an API key of the surface itself, as does a Caller Context API Key secret named like a key on a surface whose inbound Identity is From API Key. A new reference to another surface's key, of any tenant, answers 400, so no Identity can derive another surface's key DID. A reference the stored surface already had is kept on save under warn. The provider key check reports such a key as other_surface, and the Surface Builder key picker lists only the surface's own active keys. A surface template drops every API key id it would carry, including an API key shaped Caller Context secret and the Identity nodes of variant canvases.
- **Refuse a surface save that adds a new reference to a revoked API key** — A create or save that adds a revoked API Key Provider key to an Identity slot, the target identity injection, a Transit Point or a legacy Caller Context secret now answers 400 "API key '<key id>' is revoked; name an active API key of this surface", so a save no longer accepts a surface that would not serve. Such a save reads the surface's own key folder from disk, so a key another node revoked is refused at once; when that folder cannot be read it answers 503 "API key store is unavailable". A key the surface may not name for another reason keeps that rule's message, so the status of another surface's key is not disclosed. A reference the stored surface already named is kept on save, and the surface stays not ready until it names an active key, which gives it a new DID.
- **Revoke the API keys of surfaces deleted before the upgrade when going Active** — When the gateway boots Active or a Standby node becomes Active, it revokes every active API key whose owning surface no longer exists, under the actor surface-delete. Before it revokes any, it logs a WARN per owner it will revoke, naming the owner and its active key ids. It then logs each revoked key with its key_id and actor=surface-delete, and how many it revoked. The revocations are permanent: no API restores a revoked key, and rolling back the gateway does not either. Keys of surfaces deleted by an earlier release stayed active, so a surface deriving its DID from one kept serving it under warn; it now answers 503 as revoked_key until it names an active key of its own, which gives it a new DID. A surface counts as existing when its file is in the agent surface folder, readable or not, or when the surface store, the static configuration or the DynamoDB source holds it; a key owner an API Key Provider of a surface names keeps its keys too, unlike a surface delete, and so does an owner whose newest key was created within cache_refresh_interval_secs (30 seconds when it is 0). When the API keys, the surface folder or the DynamoDB source cannot all be read, or no surface is found at all, nothing is revoked. A Standby node revokes nothing.
- **Refuse saving a surface route that could take another surface's traffic** — A surface route could contain `$` or `%24` and spell another surface's variant URL, or end in extra slashes and outrank a more specific surface, so requests meant for the other surface reached it. The admin API now refuses a new or changed route that is not a plain path, and the gateway ranks surfaces by their route without trailing slashes. A stored route like that keeps serving as before; the gateway logs a warning for it at startup, so move it or disable the surface.
- **Close SSRF and DNS rebinding gaps in JWKS fetches and provider token calls** — A JWT verification strategy's JWKS fetch allowed loopback hosts, credential provider token requests honoured an HTTP proxy that resolved the host again, and the credential provider validation probe sent its request through a client that resolved the host again after it was checked. JWKS fetches, token requests and both probes now go through a pinned client that refuses cloud metadata, loopback and unspecified addresses, connects only to the address it checked so a host cannot rebind to a refused one, and follows no redirects. Private networks stay allowed, so an identity provider inside the operator's network keeps working. The JWKS answer is read up to 256 KiB. The JWKS probe and the provider probe now answer fixed error messages instead of the upstream error, and log the detail.
- **Keep listeners bound while surface changes are applied** — Saving an inactive surface, a surface with variants or Transit Points, or deleting a surface with variants reloaded the whole configuration by stopping every port listener, the management API's included, and binding it again, so connections were refused for up to half a second on every such save, and anyone allowed to edit surfaces could keep the gateway refusing connections for every tenant. A reload now builds new routes and swaps them into the listeners already bound, which stay up, and binds a port again only when its addresses or TLS setting change in gateway.json. A port whose routes a reload cannot rebuild keeps serving its previous ones, and POST /v1/config/reload names it in its warning.
- **Upgrade rmcp to 3.9 and rmcp-openapi to 0.32.1** — Before 3.9.0, the Streamable HTTP clients in rmcp read a response body into memory with no size limit (RUSTSEC-2026-0462). The gateway already leaves those client features off and listed the advisory as ignored; it now moves to a release with the fix, rmcp 3.9 with rmcp-openapi 0.32.1, the rmcp-openapi line that uses rmcp 3, and the advisory is no longer ignored. The rmcp-openapi releases in between also change how it builds tools from an OpenAPI document (OpenAPI examples, allOf and anyOf in tool schemas, deepObject query parameters sent as key[prop]=value). tokio-util is no longer pinned to an exact version, since rmcp-openapi 0.32 needs 0.7.19 or later.

### Added

- **⭐ Charge surfaces through agent-pay with an agent_pay payment policy** — A surface now charges through a `payment_policy` of type `agent_pay` that names a payment surface on a connected payment gateway. This gateway decides whether a request is chargeable (MCP tool triggers, A2A method and message filters, or `require_all`; a policy with no trigger for its protocol charges every `tools/call`, or `message/send` and `message/stream`), and asks agent-pay to take the payment only after gateway and surface policy, just before forwarding, on the direct Access Point, `fabric://`, MCP proxy and A2A proxy Targets, and Fabric receive. A free request makes no fabric hop. agent-pay gets a small payment request and a `payment_context` (payer, intent, idempotency key, trace), never the caller's body, headers or query string. Challenges, refusals, holds (`202` / MCP `1003`), rate limits and outages map to protocol-specific responses, including `payment_unconfirmed` with `Retry-After` when agent-pay cannot say whether it took the payment and `409 payment_in_progress`; receipts come back as `PAYMENT-RESPONSE` or `Payment-Receipt`, in the JSON-RPC result and in A2A message or task metadata, on every response path. A JSON-RPC batch on a charging surface is refused.
- **Let one payment credential pay for exactly one forwarded request** — A credential agent-pay answers as paid again for a request this gateway already forwarded is answered as unpaid with a fresh challenge and reason `payment_already_used`. The idempotency key sent to agent-pay covers the request (method, path, variant and the body without any credential), so one credential on another tool or other arguments is another payment. A credential is handed back, so the client can retry it within agent-pay's replay window, when its request fails before dispatch or no attempt reached the Target; a request whose first attempt the Target answered keeps it used, even when a retry then fails to connect.
- **⭐ Add payment gateways, with a Payment Gateways tab and connect wizard** — A remote gateway record now has a `role`, `peer` or `payment`; only a payment gateway takes payments and it is never a `fabric://` route. Payment gateways have their own routes, `/v1/payment-gateways/*`, permissions, `payment_gateways.view|edit|delete`, and appliance limit, `connections.paymentgateways`. Connections has a Payment Gateways tab whose wizard connects to agent-pay, waits for agent-pay's administrator to approve, and checks the peer with agent-pay's `gateway-info`; each payment gateway has a page with its payment surfaces, the surfaces that charge through it and a ping, and a connected peer can be turned into one with Use as payment gateway (`POST /v1/gateways/{id}/payment-role`, `409` while a surface routes to it over `fabric://`). Disabling or deleting a gateway is refused with `409` while surfaces charge through it or it holds unsettled payments, and a payment gateway with `503` while the payments ledger is not available to tell.
- **⭐ Capture held agent-pay payments only after the Target delivers** — When agent-pay only holds the funds (`payment-authorization`), the gateway captures them through a fabric `payment-settle` once the Target delivered and voids them otherwise, writing the decision to the ledger first. A streamed response is returned at once and captured as soon as a JSON-RPC result (any body bytes, without JSON-RPC) has been passed to the caller or the stream completes, so a caller that reads the result and disconnects still pays; an event stream ends with a `payment-receipt` event. A reconciler on the Active node resends capture or void decisions, voids stale holds, settles unconfirmed rows through `payment-settlement-status` starting with held payments, closes a cancelled request agent-pay never took a payment for as `no_payment_taken`, and raises `payment.reconcile_failed` after the deadline.
- **Add the payments ledger and its admin API** — Every payment gate call is recorded in an append-only ledger of hourly segments, kept `payment_ledger.retention_days` (default 7) and bounded by `payment_ledger.max_size_mb` (default 2 GiB); neither deletes a payment still awaiting capture or void. Money rows are synced before the request goes on, and the gateway writes the rest during its shutdown. `GET /api/admin/payments`, `/{id}`, `/summary`, `/breakers/{id}` and `/audit` need `payments.view` and are tenant scoped; a held payment's detail adds the live `case_status` agent-pay reports for its case.
- **Discover each payment surface's Payment Manifest** — Saving an `agent_pay` policy fetches the payment surface's Payment Manifest over the fabric and stores it as `manifest_snapshot`; an unreachable gateway saves with a `payment_unverified` warning, keeping a snapshot already verified. `GET /v1/payment-gateways/{id}/surfaces/{surface_id}/payment-manifest` returns one. The snapshots are refreshed every 15 minutes when what they offer changes, with price drift logged, and the ledger flags a receipt price that differs from the manifest.
- **Show agent-pay payment method logos in the Payment manifest preview** — agent-pay names each payment point's logo in the Payment Manifest and sends the logo beside it. The gateway keeps only referenced SVG logos that match their SHA-256 and pass a safety check, stores at most 1,024 of them by hash, deletes those no stored manifest references an hour after they were written, and serves them from `GET /v1/gateways/payment-logos/{sha256}` (`payment_gateways.view`) as inert images; the Payment element's preview shows them next to each method, or a generic icon.
- **Add payment events and payment gate metrics** — Ten `payment.*` events (`challenge_issued`, `paid`, `held`, `rejected`, `unconfirmed`, `unavailable`, `authorized`, `voided`, `capture_failed`, `reconcile_failed`) are part of the integration event catalog and carry no payer identity or credential; an appliance-wide `payment` integration from an earlier build keeps receiving all of them. The gate's request count and latency are exported to Prometheus, OpenTelemetry and CloudWatch.
- **Add a per-gateway payment breaker with configurable thresholds** — A payment gateway that fails is refused with `503` and `Retry-After` until one probe finds it back. `payment_gate.breaker_failures` and `breaker_open_secs` in gateway.json (defaults 5 and 30) set it, and only agent-pay transport failures count, not a caller's own short deadline.
- **Select MCP tools from the Target's live list into the trigger patterns** — The Payment triggers editor of an MCP surface edits one visible pattern list with a switch that says whether the selected tools and patterns are charged or free. Ticking a tool, from a list the Target's tools fill on demand or added by name, adds its exact pattern. `POST /v1/mcp-proxies/discover-tools` now also returns each tool's `description` and, for remote servers, `read_only`, and accepts `surface_id` to list a saved MCP surface's own Target under the forward step's egress policy.
- **Show when a remote gateway's peer was last heard from** — `GET /v1/gateways` now reports `peer_last_seen_at` for a remote gateway: when one of its listeners last received an authenticated message from the peer or a pong to a ping. The Gateways list shows it under the status, so a gateway whose own mediator link is connected no longer looks healthy while its peer has gone silent.
- **Let config.toml set the VP Audit Log's rotation size and retained files** — A new startup-only `[audit]` block sets `vp_audit_log_max_bytes` (8 MiB by default; `0` switches rotation off, and a value between 1 and 65535 fails startup with `audit.vp_audit_log_max_bytes must be 0 or at least 65536 bytes`) and `vp_audit_log_keep` (`0` by default, which keeps every rotated file). Set `vp_audit_log_keep` to a positive number to bound the history on disk: only the newest that many rotated files are kept and the records in the rest are deleted outright, so forward anything that must outlive them to a Governance Audit integration first.
- **Show the counts of an incomplete audit read as lower bounds** — An audit response now says what a read did not return, in two fields. `truncated` means it stopped before the end of the history: its scan limit — `vp_audit_log_max_bytes` × (`vp_audit_log_keep` + 2) bytes, at most 256 MiB, which an appliance keeping its whole history reaches once that history passes 256 MiB — or a rotated file that is missing or unreadable. `dropped_records` counts the records in the history it could not read, such as the half-written line an append interrupted by a crash leaves behind. Either makes the counts lower bounds, so the Audit page and the Credentials audit tab show every number from that read with a trailing `+`: `1243+` records, `Page 5 of 687+`. A partial count is no longer presented as a complete one, and an Audit page that finds nothing in such a read says the records it searched held none instead of saying the log is empty and offering to turn auditing on. `GET /api/admin/payments/audit`, which has no dashboard view, carries both fields for its own counts.
- **Show how large the VP Audit Log has grown on the Audit page** — The Audit page shows the size of the audit history on disk — the live log and every rotated file — against the new, advisory `[audit] vp_audit_log_soft_cap_bytes` (1 GiB by default; `0` shows the size alone). It enforces nothing: no record is deleted, no write is refused and the log keeps growing past it. It is there because the default retention never deletes anything, so the growth is worth watching; past the size the page names the two things an operator can do about it. About the Audit Log explains what the size covers and that the advisory size enforces nothing, so a filling bar does not read as a deadline.
- **Let a Target release a person's grants to the partner requests sent for them** — A surface's Target can set delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers, off by default and overridable per variant, where a complete variant's Target override that omits it turns it off. On a view whose HTTP or HTTPS Target sets it, and whose caller authentication tells a partner's users apart (a JWT that names an audience, or a resource token), a legacy Fabric request gets the per-user grant of the caller the receiving surface verified, as a direct call does, instead of a 403 delegation_not_enabled_for_partners. Such a view keeps each person's grant under the surface, on legacy direct calls too, so every agent acting for that person shares one grant, and nothing the partner sends, such as an agent, a DID or a transit token, selects a grant. Two Agent Gateways cannot use the switch yet, as an Agent Gateway peer strips its caller's token on legacy Fabric sends, and it must never be paired with a partner that presents a static target_auth JWT, which maps every one of that partner's users to one grant. The person connects by calling the surface directly; consent is never started through a partner, and a caller with no grant is refused with 403 consent_not_available_for_partners. The switch is read on every request, and a node that predates it refuses to load a surface that sets it, so turn it off everywhere before moving back to such a release.
- **Warn when a Target releases grants where callers cannot be told apart** — A save of a surface whose Target sets delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers, on the surface or in an enabled variant, answers with a Warning header for each such view whose caller authentication cannot tell a partner's users apart, such as an API key, an API Key Provider key, DID Auth or a JWT without an audience, whose JWT verification strategy names no issuer, or whose Target is not an http:// or https:// URL, to which Fabric receive sends no delegated credential. Fabric receive releases none of that view's per-user grants. The save is not refused, as for a Transit Point that sets the same switch.
- **Add the Release per-user grants to Fabric callers switch to the dashboard** — The Managed Agent panel shows a Release per-user grants to Fabric callers switch for an HTTP or HTTPS Target on a surface with credential bindings. It is off by default, writes target.delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers, round-trips through variants, and warns while on that a paired gateway sending a person's own token gets that person's grant. A dashboard save drops the field for any other Target.
- **Serve a Transit Point whose target is an MCP Proxy** — A Transit Point whose target_endpoint is proxy://<id> was forwarded as if the id were a host name, so every call failed. It is now answered by that MCP Proxy, one the surface may reference: initialize and tools/list from its spec, a notification accepted with nothing sent, and a tools/call through its REST backend, under the Transit Point's own timeout (or the surface's) and mcp_http limits; any method but POST answers 405 and a 2026-07-28 request is refused with -32022 before any credential is prepared. Every tool call, with a grant or without, goes through pinned Strict egress: https only, internal addresses refused after DNS resolution, the connection pinned, redirects refused and no HTTP proxy. Such a Transit Point speaks MCP and sends its backend no target_auth and no delegated credential but a consent_mode external grant: a surface save refuses one that breaks this, on the surface and in every variant, and one stored outside the API is refused when called (503), before any credential is read. A surface save now checks that a Transit Point may reference the MCP Proxy it names, using the whole id after proxy:// as a call does, and refuses an id an MCP Proxy cannot have, such as proxy://drive/x.
- **Send an external consent grant through a Transit Point's MCP Proxy** — A Transit Point whose target is proxy://<id> may now carry a consent_mode external binding, so a vendor with a REST API and no MCP server of its own, such as Google Drive, gets a per-person grant the gateway keeps, refreshes and revokes, in place of a fixed token. Only a tool call is released the grant and carries it, in its header, to the proxy's backend; initialize, tools/list and notifications are answered without it, even before the person has connected, and the token_injected audit record and the grant's last use are written only when a tool call that carries it went out to the backend, never for one its URL or Strict egress refused. A backend 401 that rejects the token gets one refresh and one retry through the proxy, and a refused grant answers connect_required, scope_insufficient or connector_unavailable, as on an https target, without the backend's words. A connector_call audit record names the class of the backend's status, not that of the MCP Proxy's answer to the agent, even when the gateway then refuses that answer, and a call no backend answered records none.
- **Answer every MCP Proxy with its grant_pin** — Every MCP Proxy API answer that returns a proxy (create, update, a GET of one and the list) now carries grant_pin: sha256: and 64 lowercase hex digits over the proxy's base_url, OpenAPI spec, flatten_post_params and name, the value a surface save sends as a proxy:// Transit Point binding's transit_credentials.mcp_proxy_pin to pin the binding to the proxy as it is. A save that changes a pin records an mcp_proxy_pin_changed audit event, in the VP Audit Log, and an info log line, with the Transit Point, the proxy and both pins.
- **List a managed identity's credential rotations from a paged endpoint** — GET /api/v1/identities/by-hash/{identity_hash}/rotations returns an identity's credential rotations oldest first, in pages of up to 1000 with limit and before, and 404 for an unknown identity. The Identities page's Version History fetches them when it opens, with loading, error and retry states.
- **Connect Kafka stream endpoints over TLS and SASL/SCRAM, as Amazon MSK needs** — A Kafka stream endpoint takes two optional fields, security_protocol (plaintext, ssl, sasl_plaintext or sasl_ssl) and sasl_mechanism (PLAIN, SCRAM-SHA-256 or SCRAM-SHA-512), so the gateway can publish to Amazon MSK with SASL_SSL and SCRAM-SHA-512. TLS verifies the brokers against the host's system CA store. An endpoint without them keeps working as before: auth_type sasl is SASL_PLAINTEXT with PLAIN (plaintext when it has no sasl_username), anything else plaintext, except that a legacy sasl endpoint stored without a password or with an empty sasl_username is now refused, since Kafka never accepted either. A save with an unknown protocol or mechanism, or a SASL protocol without a mechanism, username or password, is refused with 400. The cached producer is rebuilt when either field changes. The dashboard's endpoint editor replaces its Authentication select with a Security protocol select offering all four protocols, adds the mechanism for a SASL one, and saves every configuration the API accepts back unchanged. See docs/INTEGRATIONS.md.
- **Add credential policy rules for https Targets and Target source authentication** — The [credential_policy] bootstrap section gains target_https and target_source_auth, each warn (the default) or enforce. The startup and reload report lists surfaces whose Target or Transit Point sends a Target credential to an endpoint that is not https (http://, fabric:// and a2a-proxy:// count as not https; a proxy:// Target counts as its MCP proxy's base_url) or from a leg that does not check its callers. An A2A or AP2 surface whose a2a-proxy:// Target sends its A2A proxy's Direct Line credential is listed under target_source_auth only, when its Access Point has no source authentication.
- **Record Target credential failures in the VP Audit Log** — Each target.auth or Transit Point target_auth credential that cannot be obtained or is withheld under enforce writes one request_rejected record with reason target_auth_failed, carrying the surface, the Transit Point when it was a Transit Point's credential, provider id, trace id, outcome (rejected or passthrough) and the token endpoint's OAuth error code. A card fetch that serves a caller's request carries that request's trace id; the dashboard's card preview has none. A request refused because it would carry the credential outside the Target base path, or because a Transit Point's credential cannot be sent as a header, is recorded too, as rejected whatever the fallback. Records never carry a token, secret, token endpoint body or error_description. The OAuth error code is kept only when it is a registered RFC 6749 or RFC 6750 code; any other value is recorded as other. Rollback: a gateway version without this change cannot read these records. It skips each one without an error, so the VP Audit Log page and its totals omit them. Each one in the audit journal is counted as corrupt and logged in one warning per batch, and an unreadable record at the end of the newest journal segment is cut off at startup. The records stay in the file and show again after the upgrade.
- **Record Target authentication changes in the VP Audit Log** — A surface create, update, patch, variant write or delete through the management API records a target_auth_changed event for each leg whose Target authentication it added, changed or removed: the Target or a Transit Point, of the base surface or a variant. The record names the management caller, the method and credential provider id before and after, and the parts a change replaced, never a secret id, header or value, so the VP Audit Log shows who changed the credential a surface sends.
- **Record a forced secret delete in the VP Audit Log** — DELETE /secrets/{id}?force=true that deletes a secret records a secret_force_deleted event, whether or not records still used it. The record names the management caller as actor, the secret id, force, and each record that used the secret by kind (surface, credential_provider, a2a_proxy or sts_client) and id, so the VP Audit Log shows who broke the credentials that depended on it. It never carries the secret value or a record's name. A delete without force, and a delete the gateway refuses, record nothing.
- **Audit each call through a connector's Transit Point** — A call through a Transit Point whose transit_credentials take a connector grant (consent_mode external) is recorded as a connector_call audit event, once for each call that reaches the grant step: the Transit Point, provider, surface and its tenant, the caller's identity hash, whose grant it is (the owner's or the caller's own), the tool an MCP tools/call names, and either forwarded with the class of the vendor's answer (2xx to 5xx) or refused with the fixed reason the caller was told and its cause. A call retried with a refreshed token is one record. The record carries no argument, token or text from the vendor, and names the caller by the hash alone. Delegation audit records gain an optional tenant_id, set on these records. connector_call is in the audit event catalog, in the Delegation group, and can be selected for the VP Audit Log and for integrations.
- **Group a surface's managed agent identities into one Identities page entry** — A surface's managed agent gets a new DID when its x-identity claim values change or the surface is pointed at a different certificate or API key. The Identities page now shows every managed DID of a surface, from the agent's responses and from its requests through any Transit Point, as one entry led by the most recently used DID with the usage summed across them. The expanded row's Version History lists the earlier DIDs, each with its Trust Score, Configure Policy and Copy link actions, and the credential rotations inside them, newest first, with the fields each one changed. from_jwt_claim identities, one per claim value, and external callers keep one row per DID. A link to an earlier DID opens its entry and marks that version.
- **Record certificate and API key rotations on managed agent identities** — Rotating a stored certificate or secret-store API key keeps the managed agent's DID, and nothing recorded it. Each managed identity record now stores the slot that created it (the protected slot or a Transit Point's own managed_identity), the credential's updated_at, and a list of rotations with their time and, for a certificate, the fingerprint, subject or issuer they replaced; the record's identity fields follow the current certificate. Identities rows carry the optional slot and rotations fields, with every rotation kept. An API key's value is not compared, so any edit of the stored key's details is listed as "Credential updated". Rotations before this release were not recorded; a certificate change found on the first use after it is recorded once, without a time.
- **Let a JWT Bearer method match its audiences against client_id or azp** — An Access Point jwt_bearer method takes an audience_claim of aud (the default), client_id or azp, so identity providers whose access tokens carry no aud, such as Amazon Cognito, can still be bound to an allow-list. Such a method binds a token only to the client it was issued to, so its callers get no per-user delegated credentials: a save refuses it beside a credential binding whose consent_mode is on_demand, pre_authorize or elicit, and external-consent and Fabric grants are withheld. The dashboard's Caller Context editor has an Audience Claim select for it.
- **Add authorization_response_issuer to authorization code providers** — An oauth2_authorization_code provider without an issuer takes an optional authorization_response_issuer, the RFC 9207 iss its authorization server sends, set through the API as an https URL with a host and no query or fragment. When set, the consent callback requires that exact iss; when unset, iss is ignored. It does not make the provider an external consent provider.
- **Offer delegation_failed as a selectable audit and integration event** — A refused OAuth consent start or callback that carried a state the gateway sealed for that stage is recorded as a delegation_failed audit event with a reason code, and governance audit sinks and integrations can now select that event, which the gateway did not produce before. A refusal without such a state, such as a forged or expired one, is only logged as a WARN, so a stream of them cannot flood an audit sink.
- **Name the scopes that made a connect or refresh too wide** — A connect refused scope_widened said only that the vendor granted more than was asked, so neither the caller of the connect API nor the operator could tell which scopes the vendor added, or which scope_comparison would fit it. The failed answer now carries widened_scopes, the scopes granted beyond those asked for as the provider compares them, and the connect's log line names them; a refresh refused for a wider grant logs them too. They are the vendor's words, so only names spelled entirely as RFC 6749 scope tokens are given (no space, quote, backslash or control character), 1 to 128 bytes each and at most 16; any other is left out. Nothing else of the vendor's answer is echoed or logged.
- **Report the status of each API key a surface names in surface responses** — A surface read and the create, update and patch responses of a surface that names an API Key Provider key id now carry a read-only api_key_references list, one key_id, status and blocking per id in the base and every variant. status is active or revoked for a key of the surface, missing when no key has the id, unavailable for another surface's key, whose status is never reported, and unknown when the API key store cannot be read or no loaded key folder holds the id while a folder cannot be loaded. blocking comes from the same judgment that refuses requests, so it is true exactly when the gateway refuses the surface for that id: for revoked and missing, for unavailable when the key is revoked or tenant_credential_isolation is enforce (not for a legacy Caller Context secret whose owning surface is gone), and never for active and unknown; it says whether the reference keeps the surface from serving. The surface's own key folder is read from disk for it, so a key another node revoked is reported revoked at once. It is computed by the gateway, so a tenant caller who cannot list API keys sees it too, and a write body that sends it back has it dropped.
- **Report whether this node serves a surface, and show when it refuses requests** — Beside api_key_references, surface responses carry a read-only serving_status: serving or not_serving as this node's kept verdict on the surface's API key ids stands, and not_judged while it keeps none. It is read from that verdict only, never from the API key store, so it is per node and eventually consistent, and it gives no reason. A write body that sends it back has it dropped. The surface page shows "This surface is refusing requests" while it is not_serving.
- **Flag an Identity whose API key was revoked or deleted in the Surface Builder** — The Surface Builder draws a red dashed ring and a short reason under a protected, external or Transit Point Identity whose api_key_references entry is blocking: a revoked or deleted key, or another surface's key that is revoked or under tenant_credential_isolation enforce, in the base and every variant. The Identity panel names that state in the key picker with a warning. The stored key stays selected, by name when it was revoked, as a disabled option that cannot be picked again once changed. Save shows that warning as an error and does not save until the Identity names an active key. The API key detail page says "This API key is no longer in use because the associated surface has been deleted." for a key whose surface was deleted.
- **Keep Save open for another surface's API key under warn** — Under tenant_credential_isolation warn, an Identity whose stored key belongs to another surface is not blocking, so the gateway keeps serving the surface. The Surface Builder now matches it: the Identity panel shows a yellow notice asking for an active API key of this surface, the canvas draws no ring, and Save saves. Under enforce the key is blocking and Save stays blocked.
- **Mark an identity Revoked when its API key or every surface it used is gone** — The dashboard identity list, on the stats response and the websocket update alike, now carries revoked, true when the API key an identity's DID comes from is revoked or deleted, or when every surface the identity is linked to was deleted, and surface_deleted for the latter. A key store or surface store that cannot be read marks nothing for that reason. The Identities page shows a red Revoked badge on the identity and a deleted surface's id as plain text with a Deleted badge instead of a link, and selecting a revoked identity, there or in the Dashboard's identity details, says it is no longer in use. Revoking or deleting a key, or deleting a surface, re-sends the affected identities in the next update; an update whose since is more than 24 hours old re-sends every identity. An identity whose own surface was deleted while another surface it used is live shows that live surface, the most recently used one.

### Changed

- **Freeze component tests and make Gherkin the acceptance test layer** — Unit tests in src/component_tests/mod.rs pin every file there to its number of tests. They fail when a file is added or gains a test, and when a test or file is deleted without lowering its pin, so the folder can only shrink; new helpers are a review rule. New observable behaviour is covered by Gherkin scenarios in tests/features/, and scripts/bdd-gherkin.py --lint runs after any feature or step-binding change. AGENTS.md also asks for every step to be written test-first, with the test seen failing for the step's reason before it passes.
- **Gate the agent-pay UI and connecting payment gateways on agent_pay_delegation** — The Payment Gateways tab and pages, Use as payment gateway, the Payment element in the surface builder palette and payment templates show only while the `agent_pay_delegation` feature flag is on (default off); a surface that already has a Payment element keeps it. While it is off the gateway refuses to connect a payment gateway or turn a peer into one with `403`; payment gateways already connected keep working. The flag can no longer be overridden in a user's own settings, and it is turned on at startup when a payment gateway already exists and nobody has set it.
- **Rewrite the Payments page around the payments ledger** — The Payments page stays at `/x402payments`, leaving `/payments` free for gateway channels, and needs only `payments.view`, whatever `agent_pay_delegation` says. It lists the payments ledger with filters and a detail view per payment, and the older x402 and MPP records below it.
- **Share one gateway picker across Managed Agent, Transit Point and Payment** — The three Surface Builder panels pick a gateway and its surface the same way, with Add Gateway opening in a new tab, distinct load-failure messages with Retry and Refresh, and a saved selection kept visible when it is not listed. The Payment element lists only payment gateways, from `GET /v1/gateways/payment-providers`, which accepts `force_refresh=true` and offers no pending or disabled gateway.
- **Charge payment last instead of before policy** — The old `agent_pay` x402 delegation relayed the whole request to agent-pay before policy. A chargeable request is now charged after every refusal that does not depend on payment: gateway and surface policy, consent, target authentication, credential delegation, the checks of an MCP or A2A proxy Target and an open Target circuit breaker.
- **Map agent-pay answers by reason and to each caller's error codes** — A `403` is a payment refusal only for `rejected_by_oversight` or `delegated_mandate_denied`; any other is `502 payment_unavailable`. The `402` problem types carry a fresh challenge, `payment-expired` is a challenge and `410` is `payment_session_gone`. An MCP request with no protocol version gets `-32042` or `-32043` with the challenge data and headers; modern MCP and A2A callers get `1001`, `1002` and `1003`. The credential is read from `Authorization: Payment`, the MCP `payment_signature` and `payment_credential` arguments, and `_meta` or A2A metadata (`org.paymentauth/credential`).
- **Give gateway-level policy the payment context** — The appliance-wide and per-gateway gateway policies now get `input.payment` (`{required, rail?, amount?, currency?}`) on the direct Access Point and on Fabric receive; before, only surface policies did.
- **Use one rail, trace id and resolved variant in every payment record** — The audit trail, the ledger, the integration events and the credentials this gateway issues agree on the rail, carry the ingress trace id, and name the resolved surface variant rather than the alias a Fabric peer sent.
- **Replace the x402 Payment Wall template with an agent-pay Payment Wall** — The built-in x402 Payment Wall template created a local x402 policy that can no longer be saved. It is now the Agent-pay Payment Wall, with the same template id, charging every MCP tool call through a payment gateway and payment surface the user selects.
- **Describe agent-pay delegation as the only payment path in the docs** — The protocol, capability, fabric, federation, deployment and glossary docs no longer describe local x402 or MPP verification, the HTTP facilitator or MPP auto-pay, and the `payment` audit event is described as the agent-pay payment lifecycle.
- **Name the agent_pay_delegation flag in the legacy local payment warning** — The startup warning for a surface with an enabled local x402 or MPP policy, and the migration notes in the configuration reference, say that connecting the payment gateway an `agent_pay` policy needs requires the `agent_pay_delegation` feature flag.
- **⭐ Rotate the VP Audit Log instead of appending to one unbounded file** — The VP Audit Log was one file that grew for the life of the appliance. The live file is now renamed aside once it reaches `[audit] vp_audit_log_max_bytes` (8 MiB by default) and a fresh one is started, so the file an audit read walks stays small. Nothing is deleted by default: every rotated file is kept beside the live one, so the appliance still holds every audit record it has ever written, in `delegation-audit.jsonl.1`, `.2` and so on with the highest the most recent. Rotation is one rename however long the history is. On an appliance upgrading with a large audit log, the existing file becomes the first rotated file whole, and audit reads examine its newest 256 MiB and report `truncated` until it has aged out.
- **Show every size in the dashboard in binary units** — The Metrics and Tasks pages formatted byte counts themselves, dividing by 1024 but labelling the result KB, MB and GB, and to different numbers of decimals on each page. Every size in the dashboard now comes from one formatter and reads in the units it is actually measured in: `312 MiB`, `1.5 GiB`.
- **Match an audit text search against the stored record only** — A text search now matches each record as it is stored, before the policy version and content hash of a policy decision written before those fields existed are filled in from its trace's signed presentation. Searching for one of those two values therefore no longer finds such a row, where the previous whole-file read did; every other search is unchanged, and a page still shows the values it filled in, within the bounds of the entry below.
- **Bound what an audit page fills in from the signed presentations** — A policy decision written before the policy version and content hash were recorded with the record takes both from its trace's signed presentation, which the previous whole-file read always found. That look-up is now a second bounded walk: it stops at the read's scan limit, and at 1024 embedded decisions across the page. Such a row can therefore come back with no policy version and no content hash, and which of them are filled in follows the order of the history rather than the row. A row recorded with the two fields — every policy decision written since they existed — shows them with no look-up at all, and filtering a read to one `trace_id` gives the walk that row alone to find.
- **Key a Fabric request's delegated credentials by the receiving surface** — Fabric receive kept its Target's delegated credentials, service tokens included, under the Target's endpoint URL. It now keeps them under the receiving surface's id, the key a person's directly connected grant has on a view that releases grants to partners. The delegation audit events of a credential released on Fabric receive name that id as their agent_did, and name the Target, the caller the receiving surface verified and, as caller.peer_gateway, the paired gateway that sent the request. Service tokens are fetched again once. Entries an earlier release stored under a Target's endpoint URL, including grants people consented to through a partner, are never looked up again; operators can revoke them from the vault.
- **Send a rotation count instead of every rotation in identity rows** — Identities rows and WebSocket identity updates carry rotation_count instead of the full rotations list, so their size no longer grows with every certificate renewal or API key edit. Rotations are kept in a per-identity history stored apart from the identity record, which the per-request usage update no longer rewrites. Records that kept rotations inline still load, and their next write moves the rotations to the history.
- **Document which Fabric agent card read carries the remote Target credential** — A Fabric card read of a Transit Point target reads /.well-known/agent-card.json with the remote Target credential and falls back to /.well-known/agent.json as a public discovery read without it, so a remote Target that serves its card only at the legacy path and requires its credential there is reported as AGENT_CARD_UNAVAILABLE.
- **Request a new service token after a Target rejects the stored one** — When a Target, including the REST backend of an MCP proxy (proxy://) tool call, answers 401 with a Bearer challenge whose error is invalid_token to a service token sent as Authorization Bearer, the gateway deletes the stored token, so the next request gets a new one. The request is not retried. Any other 401, a 403, static or API key credentials, and a token sent in another header leave the stored token unchanged. A refused token is dropped only while the vault still holds that same token, no renewal is running and the token is at least 30 seconds old, and the retry backoff is kept, so a misbehaving Target cannot make the gateway request a token on every call. Dropping it removes only that token: staged consents and open MCP subscriptions are kept. A Transit Point's target endpoint and the Fabric send leg do not drop the token.
- **Stop offering From API Key on the Managed Agent's response identity** — The Identity element on the Managed Agent to Access Point response leg no longer offers From API Key in its Identity Extraction Type dropdown; the inbound and external identity slots still do. Surfaces already using it keep resolving the Managed Agent's DID from the bound API key.
- **Keep an owner's connected account when the vendor refuses its token** — A vendor's 401 to an owner binding's grant that cannot be refreshed no longer removes and revokes the grant; the call answers connect_required and the grant is kept, since any caller of the binding can send a request the vendor refuses. A caller's own grant is still removed and revoked, and a refresh token the authorization server refuses still removes either. The daily sweep removes an owner's grant once it has actually expired.
- **Document what a system that runs the connect API must do** — The connect API section of CREDENTIAL_DELEGATION.md now states the calling system's duties: name the signed-in person as the principal, bind the authorization request's state to that person's browser session, and start an owner connect only for the binding's owner.
- **Deprecate JWT Bearer methods stored without audiences** — A JWT Bearer method stored without audiences keeps working and skipping the audience check, but the gateway now warns about it at startup and in a Warning header on each save of its surface. A save that keeps it unchanged is accepted, including one that adds, copies or renames a variant inheriting it from the surface or an enabled variant; one that changes it, or enables a variant that alone has it, must name an audience. Such methods will be refused in the next major release, 2.0.0.
- **List a managed agent shared by several surfaces once, with its surfaces** — A managed agent DID used on several surfaces with the same identity is shown once on the Identities page as "Unnamed Agent" with "N surfaces" beneath, and its Agent Surface column lists every surface where it is the managed agent. The dashboard identity list (`/v1/dashboard/stats` and its live updates) gains `managed_surfaces`; `surface_id` and `surface_name` are set only for a DID with one managed surface, and `trust_recorder` of a shared DID is the worst outcome across its managed surfaces, with `trust_recorder_surface` naming the surface it came from; the Recorder chip names that surface too.
- **Show the name and the DID in separate Identities columns** — The Identities table has a Name column and a DID column with its copy button. A row without a display name shows `metadata.agent_name`, else "Unnamed Agent". The Usage Count column left the table; the usage count is still in the row details and the API. Search no longer matches a managed row by the last surface it called. A `metadata.agent_name` that is not a string is ignored instead of breaking the table.
- **Read a partner's agent card over Fabric less often** — A Transit Point policy with require_agent_context now takes the target's agent card from the card cache the Trust Checks use, instead of reading it on every request. As for Trust Checks, it may see a card up to 60 seconds old, and a card that could not be read is remembered as missing for 10 seconds, for https targets too. A Fabric card read also stops at the first path when the partner gateway refuses the request itself with 404 or 503 (an unknown or inactive surface), instead of asking the legacy path too.
- **Let an unrestricted admin read an API key whose surface was deleted** — GET /v1/api-keys/{agent_id}/{key_id} now answers an unrestricted caller for a key whose surface no longer exists, instead of 404, and every 200 answer carries surface_deleted, true when the key's surface was deleted, so the dashboard can say the key is no longer in use. Tenant-, resource- and header-gated callers still get 404 for such a key.
- **Delete an API key a surface uses after confirming it in the dashboard** — Deleting an API key that a stored surface uses is allowed. The Secrets page and the API key page open a confirmation dialog before every API key delete, saying that callers presenting the key are refused and that surfaces using it stop working until another key is picked, and delete only after Delete API key is clicked in that dialog. DELETE /v1/api-keys/{agent_id}/{key_id} removes the key, also one whose owning surface is gone, and a key that is not under the surface named in the path answers 404. Every surface naming a deleted key answers 503 until it names another key of its own, which gives the surface a new DID.
- **Confirm an API key revoke in a dialog that names its effect on surfaces** — Revoke on the Secrets page and on the API key page now opens a confirmation dialog instead of revoking at once, since a revoked key takes the surfaces using it out of service. The dialog says those surfaces stop serving until another key is selected, that a revoked key can't be restored, and that selecting a new key gives the surface a new DID. The key is revoked only after Revoke API key is clicked; Cancel and close send nothing, and a failed revoke shows an error toast.
- **Build the gateway with Rust 1.99.1, now the minimum version** — Building from source now needs Rust 1.99.1 or newer; Cargo refuses older toolchains. CI, release builds and the Docker image build on 1.99.1 instead of 1.95.0, which brings the compiler miscompilation fixes and the Cargo and libssh2 security fixes released since 1.95.

### Fixed

- **Charge agent-pay surfaces reached over the fabric** — A surface delegating payment to agent-pay was never charged when it was reached over the fabric. Fabric receive now runs the same payment gate as the direct Access Point.
- **Keep a Transit Point's gateway route in step with the picked gateway** — Choosing another gateway on a Transit Point routed via Gateway Connection now clears the previous `fabric://` endpoint and its names instead of saving the old route, and a route saved through the API opens with its gateway and surface selected.
- **⭐ Keep the Audit page from running the gateway out of memory** — Opening the Audit page, the Credentials audit tab or the payments audit view read and parsed the whole VP Audit Log on every request, so the gateway could be killed for using too much memory once the log had grown. A read now walks the history backwards one line at a time, on a blocking thread and one read at a time, and keeps only the page it returns, so it costs the same memory whether the history is 1 MiB or 1 GiB. Filters and counts are applied as each record is met, and a signed presentation is decoded only for the rows a page shows.
- **Answer an unreadable VP Audit Log with an error instead of an empty page** — The Audit page showed an empty log when the gateway could not read the audit file, asserting that an administrator was seeing all of the evidence when it had seen none of it. Both audit endpoints now answer a failed read with 500 and the JSON body `{"error": "Failed to read the audit log"}`, what failed staying in the gateway's own log. The Audit page and the Credentials audit tab show that failure, with a Retry, in place of their empty state, so a read that returned nothing is never passed off as a log that holds nothing. A live log that is not a regular file at all — a directory, a FIFO or a socket left at its path — is answered the same way, rather than returning the rotated history as though it were the whole log. A rotated file that cannot be read no longer fails the read at all: the records behind it are still returned, and the response is marked `truncated`.
- **Show how many records each audit category holds** — The Audit page's Category filter counted every category and then showed the number for none of them: a category with records displayed no count at all, and only an empty one was marked, so the only number on the row was a zero. The count now shows on each category, as a lower bound with a trailing `+` when the read did not reach the end of the history. The Flow and Type filters were given a count of zero for every option even though the gateway sends no count for them, which read as "none of these" and hid every option behind the overflow menu; they now show no number at all.
- **Accept a surface save that repairs a variant that cannot be resolved** — A save of a stored surface with a variant that cannot be resolved, such as two variants with one alias, was refused while the Target authentication rules listed the stored surface's breaches. The stored surface now keeps no breach, so a save that repairs the variant is accepted and its Target authentication is held to every rule.
- **Send only A2A and AP2 requests received over Fabric to an A2A proxy** — A request received over Fabric for an MCP or DIDComm surface whose Target is a2a-proxy:// was sent to the A2A proxy, with its Direct Line credential, although the direct path never dispatches such a surface to it and surface saves and the startup report check only A2A and AP2 surfaces for it. Fabric receive now dispatches to the A2A proxy only for an A2A or AP2 surface, as the direct path does; any other surface's request fails without reaching the proxy.
- **Answer a Fabric-received MCP proxy Target credential failure with problem+json** — A tool call of an MCP proxy received over Fabric whose Target credential cannot be resolved, with fallback reject, now answers a 502 application/problem+json like the other legs. It answered a bare error and status body before.
- **Send the Target credential over Fabric only for the card fetch, not discovery** — The receiving gateway of a Fabric route treated every public discovery GET or HEAD, /discovery included, as the sending gateway's own agent card fetch, so it sent the surface's Target credential for an unvetted caller, even under target_source_auth enforce. Only a GET of exactly /.well-known/agent-card.json or the surface's configured card location now counts as that fetch; any other discovery read gets no credential.
- **Judge an MCP proxy Target by its proxy's base_url under target_https** — A proxy:// Target never met target_https, so under enforce its credential was withheld and its save refused however secure the MCP proxy's backend was. The credential goes to the MCP proxy's base_url, so the save check, the credential policy report and every MCP proxy request now judge that URL; a missing MCP proxy still counts as not https.
- **Start the gateway when a prefix in gateway.json would hide a dashboard page** — A gateway whose gateway.json had a channel or proxy route on a dashboard page refused to start, so one bad prefix took down every surface and the dashboard with it, with no way to fix it from the dashboard. It now starts and serves everything else, leaves out each such prefix, and logs an error naming the prefix and the page it would hide. A configuration reload does the same and reports the skipped prefixes in its `warning`, and the dashboard no longer offers a skipped prefix as a Surface Prefix. Rename the prefix, for example to `/svc/<name>`, and reload.
- **Return binary REST tool answers as embedded resources with their bytes** — An MCP Proxy tool call sent by the gateway's own REST client (a 2026-07-28 call, or a legacy call carrying the Target credential) failed with "REST tool execution failed" whenever the REST API answered a body that was neither an image nor UTF-8 text, such as a PDF or a zip file. Such a body is now an embedded resource whose blob holds the bytes in base64 and whose mimeType is the response's Content-Type (application/octet-stream when it sent none), named by the backend URL without its query. The bytes decide, not the Content-Type: a UTF-8 body is answered as text as before whatever its type, so JSON served as application/octet-stream still yields structured results, and an empty body is the status text. A tool with a JSON output schema still answers a file as a schema mismatch. The body's bytes count against max_response_bytes before encoding, and a 2026-07-28 call also bounds its whole response, base64 included, by the same limit, so a file there can be at most about three quarters of it.
- **Replace the Identities page version history button, which never appeared** — The button waited for a uuid field the backend never sends, and its modal read a response shape the history endpoint does not return. The button and modal are removed; an identity's versions are listed in its expanded row instead.
- **Stop concurrent requests from losing identity usage counts** — Two requests that used the same identity at once could each read the record and save it, so one request's usage count, channel usage or origin was lost. Within one gateway process, usage, origin and rotation updates to one identity record, including those for external callers, are now serialised per identity hash.
- **Stop a Target grant test racing the revocations it asserts** — A Target that refuses a token and an authorization server that then refuses the refresh end the connection and revoke both tokens, and the revocation is spawned, so it outlives the response the caller gets. The test covering it waited a fixed 300 milliseconds for those two revocations to arrive, which is long enough on a developer machine and not always long enough on an instrumented coverage run, where the suite reported no revocation at all. It now waits for the two to arrive, up to five seconds, and asserts the same thing about them. No behaviour changed.
- **Retry the gateway-to-gateway image pulls when a registry refuses one** — The gateway-to-gateway scenarios pull a mediator image and a Valkey image before they run, and both registries throttle anonymous pulls per source address, which hosted CI runners share. One refusal failed the whole job. Each pull is now attempted up to four times, waiting 15, 30 and 60 seconds, and the job fails only if every attempt is refused.
- **Keep grants whose surface or provider cannot be read during the daily sweep** — The sweep took a surface or credential provider whose file is stored but could not be read, for example one that fails to decrypt, for one that was deleted, and removed and revoked its grants. It now skips that binding until the next sweep, as the documentation says. The revocations kept for an unreadable provider wait too, and those of every other provider are still decided.
- **Revoke a deleted provider's kept revocations before the provider is gone** — Deleting a credential provider revoked its stored grants but left the revocations kept for its earlier removals, pending or deferred to the daily sweep, which the sweep then forgot unrevoked once the provider was gone. The delete now decides them while it still holds the provider, and answers 503 and keeps the provider, naming the cause, while any revocation the vault kept cannot be read.
- **Record an Access Point grant's use at most hourly, off the request path** — A grant released for the Access Point Target was written back to the vault on every request to record its use. Like a Transit Point's, it is now recorded on a task of its own and only when the last recorded use is an hour old, and the vault checks that again before writing.
- **Revoke one binding's swept grants at a time** — The daily sweep started each binding's revocations without waiting for them, so the number of vendor calls in flight was not bounded overall. It now finishes one binding's revocations before it goes on to the next, and stops between bindings once the node is no longer active.
- **Keep revoking a deleted provider's grants that the vendor could not take** — A revocation kept pending now carries a copy of its provider's configuration, so a revocation that could not reach the vendor when the provider was deleted is tried again by the retry and the daily sweep instead of being forgotten. A vendor that answers 429 or a 5xx is retried like one that is unreachable.
- **Decide a provider delete on the provider as it is once the lock is held** — A provider delete that waited for the lock decided on the provider it had read before waiting. It now reads the provider again, so a delete of a provider already removed answers 404, and two creates of one provider slug at the same moment no longer both succeed.
- **Stop counting caller-only and deleted surfaces as a managed agent's surfaces** — Each surface use now records whether the DID was the managed agent or a caller there. A managed agent that called another surface, or whose old surface was deleted, is no longer treated as shared: the dashboard names it, and its surface name is signed into its VC and published to the Trust Registry again. A surface counts as managed only while it exists and still issues a managed identity (an `a2a-proxy://` target, or a managed identity configured on the surface), so moving or removing a surface's managed identity also clears it. Usage saved before this change follows the same rule, so an older entry for a surface where the DID was only a caller still counts until the DID calls that surface again. A surface that switches its managed identity to another credential keeps its old DID as managed until the surface is deleted or stops issuing a managed identity.
- **Retry a partner's agent card at once after a Fabric connection gap** — A Transit Point card read that failed on this gateway's side, because the Fabric connection was not up yet, the mediator refused the message or the reply could not be picked up, is no longer cached as a missing card, nor is a 503 from a briefly unavailable partner. Requests no longer report AGENT_CARD_UNAVAILABLE for 10 seconds after a gateway restart or a partner reconnecting. A partner that answers without a card, or does not answer in time, is still cached for 10 seconds.
- **Stop a Fabric Access Point calling itself for its agent card** — An Access Point with a fabric:// Target whose surface has a policy gave that policy the agent card by requesting it from its own listener on every request. It now builds the card in process, as the card preview does, so payment gating and rate limits no longer apply to that read and it no longer adds a discovery connection to the metrics. The card is read from the partner over Fabric; a card larger than a2a.max_body_size is now refused, and the read waits a2a.timeout_seconds or the surface's shorter request timeout, like a card read over HTTP. The card an Access Point serves over Fabric is read the same way, rather than with a fixed 30-second wait.
- **Hide the agent card location override on Fabric Transit Points** — The Surface Builder no longer offers Override Agent Card Location on a Transit Point whose destination is another gateway, where the gateway reads the card at the partner's discovery paths and never used the override. A stored value is kept and still ignored.
- **Read the API key store once per boot or reload for the provider key check** — Boot and a configuration reload now check every surface that derives a DID from an API key against one listing of the API key store, instead of listing the whole store once per surface, and their credential policy report judges from the same listing. When that listing fails, the report logs it once and lists each surface naming a key id as store_unavailable instead of missing_key. When it leaves key folders out, the report names them and lists a key id no loaded folder holds as store_unavailable.
- **Stop re-reading an unreadable API key store on every request** — While the API key store cannot be read, a surface naming an API key id keeps its 503 verdict until the recheck interval passes, as a missing key's verdict is kept, so a failing disk or share is read at most once per interval per surface.
- **Treat surface routes that differ only by a trailing slash as one route** — A new or changed route is stored without a trailing `/`, and routes that differ only by one count as the same route, when saving and when the gateway starts. Of two stored active surfaces like that on one listener, startup keeps the one that received the requests, the one whose stored route ends in `/`, and disables the other.
- **List secrets of every type in the API Key (secret store) picker** — The Secret ID list of a Caller Context or Transit Point caller using API Key (secret store) showed only secrets of type API Key, so a secret saved with the default type General never appeared and nothing said why. The gateway reads the secret whatever its type, and the Secrets docs call the type descriptive only, so the list now offers every secret, those of type API Key first.

### Removed

- **Stop serving the x402 HTTP facilitator and test endpoints** — `/api/x402/*` and the x402 test endpoints are no longer mounted, whatever `facilitator_mode` says. The settlement and recovery workers keep running so x402 payments verified earlier can still settle.
- **Stop acting as an x402 facilitator over the fabric** — A peer's fabric `x402/verify-request` is answered `valid: false` and an `x402/settle-request` `settled: false`, both with `facilitator_disabled`, whatever `facilitator_mode.facilitator_via_fabric` says; a warning is logged at startup when it is set.
- **Remove local x402 and MPP payment code that can no longer run** — Local x402 and MPP verification, MPP auto-pay, the MPP posture check on save and the agent card paywall are deleted, since every surface with a local policy is already refused with `503 payment_unavailable`. `x402-proxy.json` is no longer read at startup and can be deleted, and the agent card preview no longer reports `payment_required`. x402 payments verified earlier still settle, and the Payments page still lists the legacy x402 and MPP records.
- **Remove the x402 and MPP editors and the Paywall tab from the dashboard** — The Payment element no longer edits local x402 or MPP settings, and the Policies page no longer has a Paywall tab, since a surface charges through its `agent_pay` policy.
- **Remove manual channel id entry and the payment-only switch from Payment** — The Payment element's payment surface is chosen only from the surfaces the payment gateway lists; a channel id can no longer be typed in, and the "Only payment connection points" switch is gone because only payment gateways are listed. A saved variant stays selected.
- **Remove the unused mpp.example.json example** — The `mpp.example.json` example is deleted: nothing reads a local MPP configuration.
- **Remove the "name conflict" badge and the "Unnamed only" Identities filter** — The badge is replaced by the shared managed DID display, and `name_conflict` is no longer returned by the dashboard identity list (`/v1/dashboard/stats` and its live updates).

## [1.0.6] - 2026-11-05

### Breaking

- **Refuse JWT bearer credential providers saved without a subject** — Creating, updating or validating an oauth2_jwt_bearer credential provider without jwt_subject now answers 400, and so does any update of a stored provider that has none, a signing key rotation included, until the request names one. Without domain-wide delegation, a Google service account's subject is its own client_email. The dashboard's credential provider editor marks Subject as required and refuses to save without it.
- **Fail requests carrying two credentials and trim rule-breaking stored methods** — A request carrying credentials for two of a surface's methods, which the first method used to verify, now fails source authentication: it is refused on a closed Access Point, with `on_failure: reject` or beside protected resource metadata, and otherwise passed to policy as `method: "failed"`. Wherever surfaces load (startup from local storage, DynamoDB or its cache, configuration or single-surface reloads, and storage events applying a surface another process wrote), a stored surface or enabled variant whose methods break the new rules (two methods on one credential location, a second DID Auth method, a body-extracted method beside another, a JWT bearer method after the first beside protected resource metadata, or an invalid later method) keeps only its first method. An active node's load persists the trim to local storage, a storage event serves it from memory, and the gateway logs an error naming each dropped method by tag, credential location and non-secret references, so it can be rebuilt. A stored `from_jwt_claim` identity beside a method other than JWT bearer, or an inbound `from_api_key` identity beside a method other than an API key, is logged as a warning at startup and refused on the next save; until then a caller verified by that other method is refused. Audit `caller_authentication.methods` before upgrading. Before rolling back to a release that enforces only the first method, remove every method but the first: that release strips only the first method's header, so it would forward a later method's credential, such as a partner's `X-API-Key`, to the Target.
- **Refuse non-MCP Transit Point bodies over a2a.max_body_size with 413** — A Transit Point without mcp_http read the request body without a limit and answered 400 only when the read failed. It now reads at most a2a.max_body_size, answers a larger body with HTTP 413 and records a request_rejected at admission with reason body_too_large; other read failures are still 400. To accept larger bodies, raise [a2a] max_body_size and restart the gateway.
- **Refuse non-MCP fabric:// Access Point bodies over a2a.max_body_size** — A fabric:// Access Point skipped the request size check. One without mcp_http now refuses a body over a2a.max_body_size with HTTP 413 as it reads it, and records a request_rejected with reason body_too_large. An MCP fabric:// Access Point keeps reading up to its mcp_http.max_request_bytes. To accept larger bodies, raise [a2a] max_body_size and restart the gateway.
- **Bound Transit Point bodies through the shared host by a2a.max_body_size** — An inbound listener that shares a host name with an outbound listener passes /outbound/... requests on to it. Through it, a Transit Point request body is now bounded by a2a.max_body_size, even for an MCP Transit Point whose mcp_http.max_request_bytes is higher: a body declaring a larger Content-Length is refused with 413 without being read, and a chunked body is cut once it is 512 KiB and one chunk past the limit, so such an MCP Transit Point still accepts a chunked body up to that size. To send larger bodies to it, raise [a2a] max_body_size and restart, or call the outbound listener's own port, where its own limit holds.
- **Stop passing cookies through Access Points and MCP Proxies** — An agent behind an Access Point no longer receives its caller's cookies or sets cookies on its callers, so cookie-based session affinity or agent-held cookie sessions stop working; use a header for affinity instead. An MCP Proxy's REST API no longer receives the caller's cookies, and a JWT bearer method with forward_header on a header named Cookie forwards nothing. Scripts that send Cookie session_token to the management API get 401; send Authorization Bearer or the __Host-session_token cookie. The dashboard, and CLI browser login, now need HTTPS or http://localhost: over plain HTTP on any other host the live-update notice keeps returning and CLI login never completes.
- **Answer 500 to a surface save naming an API key id without an API key store** — Without an API key store the gateway cannot tell whose key an `atgk_` id is, so saving a surface that names one, including as a Caller Context API Key secret on a view whose inbound Identity is From API Key, now answers `500` "API key store is not configured" instead of saving it. Fix the API key store before saving such surfaces.
- **Refuse a new reference to an API key that does not exist** — A `from_api_key` identity or identity injection whose `atgk_` key id names no key, or a key whose owning surface was deleted, is refused on save with `400`, where it used to be saved with a DID derived from the id alone. A surface that already stored the reference keeps it, so a GET then PUT still saves. Issue a new API key to add one.
- **Reserve the atgk_ prefix for API key ids and refuse it as a new secret id** — A secret named after an API key id shares that key's DID. `POST /v1/secrets/new` now refuses a `secret_id` that starts with `atgk_` with `400`. Secrets already stored with such an id keep loading and serving; replace them with a secret of another id to stop sharing a key's DID.
- **Match a resource-scoped token's pattern against the API key ids a surface names** — Saving a surface with a resource-scoped access token now needs each API Key Provider key id the surface names to match the token's pattern as `api-keys:<key id>`, also for a key the stored surface already named. Widen the pattern of tokens that save such surfaces.
- **Refuse a tenant surface naming the API key of a surface with no tenant** — A tenant surface naming the key of a surface with no tenant is held to `tenant_credential_isolation`, as an appliance-wide secret sent upstream is: a new reference is refused on save, and one the stored surface already named is kept under `warn` and refused under `enforce`.
- **Answer 503 from stored surfaces naming an API key they may not use when enforced** — Under `tenant_credential_isolation: enforce`, a stored surface naming another tenant's API Key Provider key, a tenant's key on a surface with no tenant, or the key of a surface with no tenant now answers every request with `503`. If you run `enforce`, upgrade with `warn` first, read the startup credential policy report for `provider_key_tenant` lines, fix those surfaces, then restart with `enforce`. Such a surface still reports `access_point_status: ready`; the gateway log line with `rule="provider_key_tenant"` says why it answers `503`.
- **Answer 503 from surfaces naming an API key id while the key store is unavailable** — In either `tenant_credential_isolation` mode, a surface naming an `atgk_` id that has not been checked answers `503` while the API key store cannot be read, where it used to serve. A store that fails to start keeps such surfaces not ready until the gateway restarts with a working store.

### Security

- **⭐ Add a unique jti to every JWT bearer grant assertion** — Every JWT bearer grant assertion now carries a random jti, so an authorization server can refuse a replayed assertion.
- **Enforce every stored caller authentication method, not only the first** — Earlier releases enforced only the first method in a stored surface's `caller_authentication.methods` and ignored the rest. On upgrade, with no operator action, they take effect, which widens who reaches the managed agent: a caller presenting only a later method's credential, which failed before (refused, or passed to policy as `source_auth.method == "failed"`), is now authenticated by that method. Audit `caller_authentication.methods`, on each surface and each variant override, before upgrading. At startup the gateway logs, at `WARN`, one line per surface and per variant with more than one method, naming the methods after the first.
- **Keep DID private keys and refresh tokens out of the support export** — The `.atgx` support export carried the private keys of Connection Point, issuer and trust registry DIDs, stored as `key_<n>.json` beside their DID documents, and the private keys inside issuer and mediator records, because redaction replaced only string values and missed `privateKeyJwk`, `secrets`, `our_secrets` and `refresh_token`. The export now leaves out every `key_<n>.json` file in plaintext, encrypted or leftover temporary form, and replaces the whole value of a secret field, whether a string, an object or a list. DID documents and the rest of each record are still exported.
- **Require settings.edit to remove rotated logs or truncate stored metrics** — `POST /v1/logs/truncate` and `POST /v1/metrics/truncate` answered any signed-in user and any personal access token, so any caller could delete the rotated `agent-gateway*.log*` files or drop stored metrics past the retention period. Both routes now need `settings.edit`, which the built-in defaults grant to administrators only, and a personal access token with feature scopes needs `settings.edit` among them. A caller without it gets `403` and nothing is removed. Without an auth backend the routes are not registered.
- **Require users.approve to read or change the welcome template or send one** — `GET` and `PUT /v1/notifications/user-welcome-template` and `POST /v1/notifications/send-welcome` answered any signed-in user and any personal access token, so any caller could rewrite the message newly approved users receive or place a notification with any title and text in any user's inbox. The three routes now need `users.approve`, which the built-in defaults grant to administrators only, and a personal access token with feature scopes needs `users.approve` among them. Without an auth backend they refuse every request with `403`. The other notification routes still act on the caller's own notifications with a session only.
- **Stop Transit Points passing a caller's client address, host and path headers** — A Transit Point dropped a caller's Forwarded, X-Real-IP and X-Forwarded headers but passed the other headers proxies and CDNs use to claim the client's address, host, path or scheme, such as True-Client-IP, CF-Connecting-IP, X-Client-IP, WL-Proxy-Client-IP, X-Envoy-External-Address, X-Original-Host and X-Original-URL, so a target that trusts the gateway as its proxy could still take the caller's claim. It now drops those too, in any letter case and with underscores for dashes, and the docs list every header.
- **Bound and escape the caller's path on the log lines of a matched route** — A request to a served route, before any caller authentication, logged its whole path at INFO when the route matched, and again on the route matching and rewriting lines and when the surface's handler took it, so a long or crafted path could flood the log or show a capability id in full. Those lines now carry the path, its tail and its alias escaped, cut to 256 characters and with capability ids shortened, like the unknown path line, and three lines that only repeated others are gone. The onboarding handler's line does the same.
- **Escape the provider id on the OAuth consent callback's log lines** — The OAuth consent callback, which needs no authentication, logged the provider id from its path as-is on every line, so a caller could put a line break in it and forge log lines. It is now escaped and cut to 256 characters.
- **Refuse an OAuth consent callback whose path names another provider** — The OAuth consent callback took the provider id from its path for its audit event and to wake the tool calls waiting on the consent, though the state it carries names the provider. A valid code and state replayed to another provider's callback path stored the right token but recorded the wrong provider and woke no waiting call.
