Such a callback is now refused before the code exchange, without using up its state.
- **Forward a caller's JWT to the Target only after it verified** — With forward_header on a JWT bearer Access Point, a token that failed verification, such as an expired one or one from another issuer, was still sent on to the Target whenever policy let the failed caller through. The token now reaches the Target only when it passed verification, and a public discovery read, which verifies no caller, forwards none. A Target that checks tokens itself no longer gets one the method's strategy rejects: make the strategy and audiences accept the tokens it needs, or give the Target its credential through target authentication instead.
- **Refuse a JWT bearer caller that sends more than one bearer token** — JWT bearer verification read only the first value of the token header, while forward_header sent every value on, so a second token could ride along with a valid one. A request whose token header carries more than one value in the method's scheme now fails verification, on an Access Point, a Transit Point and over Fabric, with or without forward_header: a closed or reject surface answers 401, and policy sees a failed caller otherwise. A value in another scheme, such as an MPP Payment credential beside the Bearer token, does not count.
- **Check a tenant connection's audience owner on every STS grant** — A tenant's STS connection was checked against the tenant that serves the requested resource only when minting MCP resource tokens. Token exchange, ID-JAG issuance and jwt-bearer redemption outside the MCP profile ran only the connection's audience allowlist, so with an empty list, or one saved before another tenant's surface declared the resource, a connection could mint a token for a resource another tenant's surface or MCP Proxy declares. Every grant now refuses such an audience; the gateway's own issuer, third-party audiences and appliance-wide connections work as before.
- **Cap request bodies read before authentication on the inbound port** — The /outbound/... hop an inbound listener runs when it shares a host name with an outbound listener read the whole request body into memory before the Transit Point authenticated the caller, buffered the whole response, forwarded an empty body when the read failed and cut streams at a2a.timeout_seconds. It now streams both ways with no overall timeout, refuses a declared length over a2a.max_body_size with 413 without reading the body, leaves a longer one to the Transit Point's own 413 and recorded refusal and stops it after the chunk that takes it 512 KiB past the limit, logs its own refusals with the caller's address, and refuses a body the caller fails to send with 400, so the Transit Point refuses an unauthenticated caller after reading little of its body. The hop's own 413 and 400 are a JSON-RPC error with no id for a request carrying an MCP-Protocol-Version header, and an A2A problem otherwise. Access Points cap the body at a2a.max_body_size as they read it, and the DID Auth challenge and authenticate endpoints at 64 KiB, so an unauthenticated caller can no longer make the gateway hold an unbounded body. An A2A or AP2 request refused this way is answered before version negotiation and is no longer counted in agent_gateway_a2a_protocol_version_total.
- **Keep caller cookies and target cookies off Access Points** — An Access Point no longer forwards its caller's Cookie header to the managed agent, directly or over Fabric, and a gateway receiving a Fabric request drops a Cookie its peer sent. Its answers no longer carry a Set-Cookie the agent sent. Access Points share their host with the dashboard, so the dashboard session cookie reached the agent, and the agent could set cookies on the dashboard's host.
- **Keep the caller's cookies away from an MCP Proxy's REST API** — An MCP Proxy no longer forwards its caller's Cookie header to the REST API it calls. MCP Proxy routes share their host with the dashboard, so the dashboard session cookie reached the REST API, and a tool that takes a cookie argument failed whenever the caller also sent a Cookie. A REST API that needs a cookie still gets it from the tool argument its OpenAPI specification declares.
- **Harden the dashboard session cookie with Secure and the __Host- prefix** — The dashboard session cookie is now named __Host-session_token and is Secure, so a browser sends it only over HTTPS or to localhost and no other host can set it. The old session_token cookie is no longer read; an open dashboard receives the new cookie on its next request, and a browser with no open dashboard signs in once. After a rollback to a release that reads only session_token, browsers sign in once again.
- **Tenant-check the API Key Provider keys a surface derives a DID from on save** — The gateway derives a DID from an API Key Provider key id (`atgk_`) without looking the key up, and a surface save did not check whose key it named, so a surface could take the DID of another tenant's key. A save now treats each key id named by a `from_api_key` identity on the protected, external or a Transit Point slot, or by a target identity injection of type `from_api_key`, on the base and in every variant, as a reference to the key's owning surface. Another tenant's key, or a tenant's key on a surface with no tenant, is refused with `400`.
- **Refuse requests to a stored surface that names an API key it may not use** — A surface stored before the save check, or changed on disk or by another node, went live without it. At boot, on reload, on the cache refresh scan, on another node's write and on the first request to an unchecked version, the gateway now checks its key ids and logs one warning with the rule `provider_key_tenant`. Under `tenant_credential_isolation: enforce` such a surface is not ready and answers `503` on direct inbound, Fabric receive and its Transit Points. While the API key store cannot be read, a surface naming a key id that has not been checked answers `503` in either mode; a store that failed to start needs a restart. When a key-owning surface changes or is deleted, the surfaces naming its keys are checked again on their next request. A legacy Caller Context API Key secret named like such a key on an inbound From API Key view is checked the same way, and a new reference to one is refused on save.
- **Fetch no agent card preview for a surface that names an API key it may not use** — The agent card preview derived and injected the surface's identity while building the card, so it could present the DID of a key the surface may not use. A surface refused for its API Key Provider keys now gets a `not_running` preview, and nothing is fetched or kept.
- **Answer 409 to a reassignment that breaks a surface's API key references** — A tenant reassignment of a key-owning surface, or of a surface naming a key, that would leave a surface naming another tenant's key, or make a tenant surface name the key of a surface with no tenant, is now a conflict (`409`), also for a key a surface names as a legacy Caller Context secret. A reassignment whose surface then fails any reference check answers `409` instead of `500`, and so do a reassignment and an ownership impact read made while a surface names an API key id and the API key store is not configured.
- **Warn when a surface or variant reaches a bound Target's vendor without its grant** — While an Access Point Target's consent_mode external binding is served, a save, such as enabling a variant, answers with a Warning header for the surface's own route and each enabled variant that sends requests to that Target's origin without the binding or a credential of its own (target.auth, another binding or forward_header). Such a view, a variant copied from the one holding the binding and then enabled, one with outbound_credentials set to an empty list, or the surface's own route beside a variant that holds the binding when no default variant serves that route, sends the vendor its callers' own headers and no grant. A caller credential counts only when every caller authentication method forwards one. The dashboard shows the warning in the save-warnings banner. The save itself is not refused.

### Added

- **Count credential provider token requests in a metric** — The new counter agent_gateway_credential_provider_token_requests_total, labelled by provider_type and outcome (success, refused, timeout or invalid_response), counts each service token request sent to a token endpoint. Cache hits and backoff waits are not counted.
- **Report stored JWT bearer providers without a subject at startup** — The startup and reload credential policy report lists each oauth2_jwt_bearer provider without jwt_subject under the rule jwt_subject, so operators can add one before the provider's next update, which now requires it.
- **Name the claim that identifies a caller on a JWT verification strategy** — A JWT verification strategy has an optional principal_claim, default sub, whose value names the caller. It is the subject of the caller's identity hash, so the transit token's user_identity_hash, the keys of delegated credentials and the connect hint follow it. Token exchange, jwt-bearer redemption and the modern consent sign-in check read the subject from it too. A claim other than sub adds its name to the identity hash and to the MCP bound subject, so one value in two claims is two callers; a strategy without the setting keeps every hash it gave. Use oid for Microsoft Entra, whose sub differs for each application, with the v2 issuer. A token without the claim, or with a value that is not a non-empty string, names no caller. The claim is fixed when the strategy is created: an update that names another claim is refused and one that leaves it out keeps it. Claims that name a token, client, tenant, session or lifetime are refused. A strategy stored with a refused claim, such as one edited by hand, is logged when it loads, a surface save warns of it, and JWT source authentication through it answers 500. An issuer names callers by one claim in a tenant scope for token exchange: STS refuses its tokens while two strategies of the scope name it by different claims, and creating or updating such a strategy, or saving a surface that accepts the issuer through both, warns. For a strategy naming another claim, the audit record keeps the token's sub and adds the claim's value as principal, which the Credentials audit tab shows. The Edit JWT Verification Strategy page has the field, read-only after creation.
- **⭐ Accept more than one source authentication method on one Access Point** — An Access Point accepted a list of caller authentication methods but enforced only the first, so an agent called by internal users with a JWT and by partner agents with an API key needed two surfaces, and two identities. Every method in `caller_authentication.methods` now works: the gateway verifies each request with the method whose credential the request carries (a non-empty header, for JWT bearer one holding a token in its scheme so an MPP `Authorization: Payment` credential is not a JWT, or the client certificate for mTLS), or with the first, primary, method when it carries none, and never retries a rejected credential with another method. A request carrying credentials for more than one method fails source authentication as the primary method, audited as `ambiguous_credentials`: it is refused on a closed Access Point, with `on_failure: reject` or beside protected resource metadata, and otherwise passed to policy as `input.source_auth.method == "failed"`. Policy sees the method that verified the caller as `input.source_auth.method`. Creating or saving a surface now validates every method, on the base and each enabled variant, and refuses two methods reading the same credential location, more than one DID Auth method, a JWT bearer method after the first beside protected resource metadata, a `from_jwt_claim` identity unless every method is JWT bearer, and an inbound `from_api_key` identity unless every method is API Key or API Key Provider. The credential headers of all methods are stripped before forwarding, and a JWT bearer method with `forward_header` forwards its header only when it was the method selected for the request. On an MCP Access Point that publishes protected resource metadata, a failed JWT bearer credential gets the `401` discovery challenge and a failed credential of another method a plain `401`, directly and over Fabric. The Surface Builder's Caller Context element lists its methods in the sidebar and in its editor, each with whether it is ready, what it reads and a Configure button that opens its settings; methods are added from a menu, and made primary or removed in the editor.
- **Limit each caller's requests on a Transit Point** — A Transit Point can set per_caller_rate_limit (requests, window_secs and an optional burst) to give each caller it identifies a rate limit of their own, so one caller cannot use up the allowance of the others. The caller is the one its transit token names, an optional token included, or else the client its source_auth verified. Requests with no identified caller share one bucket, the number of buckets is bounded (a caller who has a bucket keeps it), and the limit applies beside the point's rate_limit. A surface variant's Transit Point is limited by the surface's per-caller limit and a variant that sets another is refused on save. A limit that cannot be enforced is refused on save, and one stored anyway makes the Transit Point answer 503. A refused request answers 429 with Retry-After, as a JSON-RPC error on an MCP Transit Point, and is recorded as a rate_limited audit event with scope transit_point_caller, the Transit Point and the caller's identity hash, or with scope transit_point_overflow and no caller when the shared overflow bucket refused it. The Surface Builder's Rate Limit panel on a Transit Point has the setting, read-only on a named variant.

### Changed

- **Pin the caller identity hash and connect hint with documented test values** — The credential delegation reference now states that the caller identity hash and the connect hint are a compatibility surface for systems that run a connect for a caller, with no pepper or setting in either, and lists fixed example values for a JWT caller (with and without a tenant, and with a subject that needs escaping), an API key caller and the hints for them. Tests hold the same values, so a change to either form fails them.
- **Refuse an a2a.max_body_size of 0 at startup** — An [a2a] max_body_size of 0 would refuse every request body. The gateway now refuses to start with it and names the setting in the error; the minimum is 1 byte.
- **Stop counting an oversize Access Point request body as a task error** — An MCP Access Point counted a request body over its mcp_http.max_request_bytes as an error of its task on the Tasks page. A body refused for its size is an admission refusal, like the other admission refusals, so neither an A2A nor an MCP Access Point counts it as a task error now; a body the caller fails to send still counts.
- **Stop showing the Access Point's save warnings in the dashboard** — Saving or creating a surface in the dashboard no longer shows the gateway's Access Point warnings (public, not ready for external access, and neither closed nor public) in the save toast or the "The last save drew a warning" banner. Turning Public access on is the operator's own choice, and the canvas and Access Point panel already show whether the Access Point is ready. Every other Warning header still shows in the toast and the banner, and one that names a Transit Point also in that Transit Point's panel. API clients still receive every Warning header.

### Fixed

- **Let a Fabric Transit Point use caller authentication and a Trust Check together** — The gateway read a fabric:// Transit Point's target agent card through the Transit Point's own listener without a credential, so the Transit Point's caller authentication refused it, the card was unavailable and every Trust Check failed. A Transit Point with no custom route never got the card at all. The card for Trust Checks and for policies with require_agent_context is now read over Fabric directly, at the well-known card paths as before, and outside callers still need a credential to read it through the Transit Point.
- **Show the Dashboard metrics Delete button only to users with settings.edit** — The Dashboard's Gateway Configuration card showed the metrics Delete button to every user, although truncating stored metrics needs `settings.edit`. The button now appears only for users who hold `settings.edit`, so others no longer see an action the gateway refuses.
- **Warn when a listener's external URL has no host that parses** — An external URL that does not parse, such as one with an out-of-range port, quietly stopped its listener sharing the outbound proxy with another listener of the same host. Startup now logs one warning for each such URL, naming the listener's id and the URL's position in its list, never the URL, so a credential in it stays out of the log.
- **Record an MCP Transit Point's refusal of an oversize body** — An MCP Transit Point answered a body over its mcp_http.max_request_bytes with 413 but recorded nothing. It now records a request_rejected at admission with reason body_too_large, as an MCP Access Point does.
- **Reload API keys when a Standby node becomes Active** — A Standby node promoted to Active kept the API keys it loaded at startup, so a key the other node created, rotated or revoked meanwhile was refused, kept its old secret, or still authenticated until a restart. On becoming Active the node now reconciles its API keys with disk before it reloads its configuration.
- **Take a surface's credential bindings from the surface, not its canvas layout** — The Surface Builder rebuilt the Credential Delegation element from the canvas layout saved with a surface whenever the surface itself held no outbound_credentials. A binding moved to a variant or removed through the API therefore came back on the next dashboard save. For an Access Point Target binding with consent_mode external, this put the binding in two places, and the gateway refused the save with 400. The element now takes its bindings from the stored outbound_credentials alone. A Credential Delegation element whose rows name no provider yet, which no save stored, is still kept through a reload.
- **Save a variant without credential bindings as having none** — The Surface Builder left outbound_credentials out of a variant that had no credential bindings on its canvas. The gateway reads a missing list as keeping the base surface's bindings, even for a complete variant, so such a variant went on sending the surface's credentials, an external consent grant included, while the dashboard showed it with none. A dashboard save now sends the variant an empty list, so it has no bindings, as the dashboard shows. A stored variant that inherits the surface's list keeps inheriting while it shows no binding and the surface has none.

## [1.0.5] - 2026-11-03

### Breaking

- **Refuse new http token endpoints when saving a credential provider** — Creating a credential provider, changing its token endpoint, or turning an API key provider into an OAuth type now answers 400 "token_endpoint must be an https URL" unless the endpoint is https or listed exactly in credential_policy insecure_token_endpoints, and the Validate check refuses such an endpoint without contacting it. Saving a stored provider that keeps its http endpoint still works while credential_policy token_endpoint_https is warn. Upgrade note: provisioning that creates or changes a provider with an internal http:// token endpoint now gets 400; use https, or list the exact URL in credential_policy.insecure_token_endpoints.
- **Refuse new credential provider references to another tenant's secrets** — Creating a credential provider, or changing a secret reference, now answers 400 "Secret reference is not accessible" unless the secret belongs to the provider's own tenant; an appliance-wide secret no longer counts for a tenant provider. Validate applies the same rule to a signing key or certificate. While credential_policy tenant_credential_isolation is warn, a stored reference is kept only in the field that holds it and while the provider's type, token and authorization endpoints, resource, additional parameters, default scopes and JWT issuer, subject and audience are unchanged, so a tenant cannot redirect an appliance-wide secret or signing key to its own endpoint. A provider with an issuer never keeps a secret outside its own tenant, even in warn mode. Upgrade note: provisioning that gives a tenant provider an appliance-wide or another tenant's secret now gets 400; create the secret in the tenant first.
- **Refuse new appliance-wide credentials on tenant surfaces when saving** — Saving a tenant surface that newly names an appliance-wide secret for static secret Target authentication, or an appliance-wide API key, client credentials or JWT bearer provider, is refused with 400, and a tenant reassignment that would create such a reference answers 409. While credential_policy tenant_credential_isolation is warn, a stored appliance-wide reference is kept only at the destination that already received it: the endpoint of its Target or Transit Point, or the caller for response metadata. A reference to a secret that does not exist is refused even when stored, and a tenant provider that holds a secret outside its tenant is refused on a new or redirected leg. Upgrade note: provisioning that names an appliance-wide secret or provider on a tenant surface, or adds a tenant provider holding an outside secret to a new leg, now gets 400, and a tenant reassignment that would create such a reference gets 409; move the credential into the tenant first.
- **Refuse every save of a tenant surface that names a missing secret** — A tenant surface whose stored custom metadata names a $SECRET: with no existing secret now answers 400 "Secret reference is not accessible" on every save, in warn mode too, even when the edit leaves the metadata alone. Upgrade note: delete or fix the reference, or create the secret in the surface's tenant, before saving such a surface.
- **Refuse tenant A2A Proxies whose Direct Line secret is outside their tenant** — Creating a tenant A2A Proxy, or changing its secret or base URL, with an appliance-wide or another tenant's Direct Line secret now answers 400 "A2A proxy secret is not accessible", in warn mode too. Upgrade note: create the Direct Line secret in the proxy's tenant, or publish the proxy appliance-wide as an operator.
- **Refuse appliance-wide secrets in a tenant token's MCP tool discovery** — POST /v1/mcp-proxies/discover-tools with a tenant token and an appliance-wide secret now answers 400 "Target auth secret is not accessible", whatever tenant_credential_isolation says. Upgrade note: run discovery with a secret of the token's own tenant, or with an operator session or appliance-wide token.
- **Fail closed when an inbound From API Key caller does not use an API key** — An inbound From API Key Identity whose first Caller Context method is not API Key or API Key Provider gave callers the stored key's DID; it now returns 500 with a fixed body. Find such surfaces through `agent_gateway_managed_identity_resolve_total` with `mode="from_api_key"` and `result` `api_key_unavailable`. Saving one, on the surface or an enabled variant, is refused with 400, as is combining it with `mcp_http.authorization`, or a From API Key Identity on the protected, external or a Transit Point slot without a key id; a stored one answers a fixed 500 (`result` `api_key_id_missing`). A missing VC issuer or a failed DID issuance still resolves no caller identity and leaves the decision to policy.
- **Give each API key its own DID, so rules tied to the shared DID stop matching** — On an API Key Provider surface, only callers presenting the stored key keep its DID; every other key gets its own. On a plain API Key surface, callers get the DID of the Caller Context secret id. That DID is new on every surface made in the dashboard, whose picker stored a provider key id, never a secret id. Policies, Trust Check entries and Transit Point OAuth grants naming an old DID stop matching, and the Transit token `sub` and Workload Binding `userIdentity.id` are per key. Re-grant or update rules for the affected keys.
- **Refuse From API Key on an MCP proxy:// surface when it is saved again** — The MCP Proxy request path never resolves the caller's key, so saving an MCP surface with a `proxy://` target and an inbound From API Key Identity is refused with 400. A stored surface keeps loading and serves its callers with no caller identity, and it cannot be saved until its inbound Identity changes.
- **Drop downgrade support for surfaces saved without an inbound key id** — A surface saved with an inbound From API Key Identity has no stored key id, which older binaries require, so they skip it at load. Downgrading after re-saving such a surface is not supported.
- **Refuse variant Transit Points that change the surface's gating or policies** — A variant's Transit Point is enforced with the `mcp_tool_gating`, `policy` and `response_policy` of the surface's Transit Point of the same alias. The admin API now refuses with `400` a variant Transit Point that sets any of them to something else, or sets one where the surface has no Transit Point of that alias. A stored variant whose Transit Point sets a different one has every request through it refused with `403`, instead of silently running the surface Transit Point's. A variant Transit Point that leaves them out keeps the surface Transit Point's. This replaces the earlier handling of gating set only on a variant's Transit Point, which refused every `tools/call` and listed no tools. Move any variant-only gating or policy to the surface's Transit Point.
- **Refuse a tool call whose `elicit` consent is declined or cannot be asked** — A tool call whose `elicit` binding was declined, cancelled or not answered within `elicit_timeout_secs`, and under `elicit_fallback: fail` one from a client without the elicitation capability or without an open SSE stream, was forwarded without the delegated credential. It is now refused as a credential that cannot be resolved: an Access Point answers `503` with a JSON-RPC error (`-32603`). A client must accept the prompt to have the call forwarded; use `elicit_fallback: on_demand` to answer a client that cannot be prompted with the consent link instead.
- **Reject a listener `listener_type` other than `inbound` or `outbound`** — A listener whose `listener_type` was anything else, `Outbound` or `egress` included, was accepted and then served as an inbound listener on some paths and ignored on others. It now stops the gateway from loading its network configuration. Write `inbound` or `outbound`, in lower case, before upgrading; leaving the field out still means `inbound`.
- **Require a transit token for discovery documents below a Transit Point's root** — A Transit Point skipped the transit token, and withheld the `target_auth` and delegated credentials, for any `GET` whose path ended like a discovery document, such as `admin/discovery` or `tools/agent.json`, and for spellings a target could resolve to another path. Only a `GET` or `HEAD` of `.well-known/agent-card.json`, `.well-known/agent.json`, `.well-known/agent-card`, `.well-known/ucp`, `agent-card.json`, `agent.json` or `discovery` directly below the Transit Point now counts. Any other path needs a transit token and carries the credentials, so a managed agent that fetched such a path without a token must now send one.

### Security

- **Check a DID Auth session against the allow-list on every request** — A DID Auth session authenticates only at the view it was minted on, the surface and the variant whose /authenticate minted it or the base surface, and every request it carries is checked again against that view's allowed_dids as they are then. A DID removed from the list, or left out of a list added after its session was minted, is refused at once rather than when the session expires, and a session minted on a variant is not taken at the base surface or another variant. session_ttl_seconds is capped at 86400, the default, and no session authenticates more than 86400 seconds after it was minted, whatever it was stored with. A save that sets a value above 86400 is refused; a stored surface above it still loads, with a startup warning, so an upgrade never stops the gateway, and can still be saved while it keeps that value.
- **Show dashboard log lines only to callers with the logs.view permission** — The dashboard delta and stats endpoints and the dashboard WebSocket returned gateway log lines to every signed-in caller, including roles and access tokens without `logs.view`. The endpoints now leave log lines out, without reading the log file, unless the caller's role and access-token scopes grant `logs.view`, and each WebSocket connection drops log updates unless its session is still live and its user is Approved and holds `logs.view`, checked on connect and on every keep-alive ping. Unauthenticated WebSocket connections and gateways without an auth backend get no log lines.
- **Refuse tenant card previews that would use appliance-wide credentials** — A tenant token that reads the agent card preview of an appliance-wide surface gets unsupported_reason operator_only with nothing fetched, and its refresh is 403, because the fetch would use the appliance's Target credential. Another tenant's surface is 404 to both.
- **Pin credential provider token requests to the vetted address** — Client Credentials, JWT Bearer and private_key_jwt grants, refreshes and authorization-code exchanges, including the 2026-07-28 consent exchange, now connect only to the address the token endpoint resolved to when it was checked, closing a DNS-rebinding gap when no HTTP proxy is configured (with HTTPS_PROXY, HTTP_PROXY or ALL_PROXY set, the proxy resolves the host itself). Redirects stay off, and a token endpoint resolving to loopback, an unspecified address or cloud metadata is refused before any request and reported as refused by egress policy.
- **Warn about http token endpoints unless credential_policy enforces https** — A credential provider whose stored token endpoint is plain http keeps working while credential_policy token_endpoint_https is warn, the default, but every use logs a warning and legacy delegation audit details gain insecure_token_endpoint. Setting credential_policy token_endpoint_https to enforce refuses such providers before any secret is read, including cached service tokens. Upgrade note: move token endpoints to https, or list a deliberate internal http endpoint in insecure_token_endpoints, before setting enforce.
- **Warn when tenants use appliance-wide credentials unless isolation is enforced** — A credential provider that reads a secret outside its own tenant keeps working while credential_policy tenant_credential_isolation is warn, the default, but each token request or API key read that uses the secret logs a warning. Setting it to enforce refuses the secret before its value is used, so no token request is sent. A tenant surface that sends an appliance-wide secret or credential provider on its Target, Transit Point, Fabric receive or delegation legs also logs a warning and notes appliance_wide_credential in the delegation audit, and enforce refuses it; another tenant's secret or provider is always refused. Upgrade note: move the secrets and providers into the tenant before setting enforce.
- **Log only the OAuth error code from credential provider token endpoints** — A refused token request, refresh, code exchange or SAML Graph API token request now logs, returns and audits only the OAuth error code of the response, or unrecognized_error when the code is missing, longer than 64 bytes or outside the RFC 6749 character set. error_description, error_uri and the rest of the body, which a provider can fill with echoed request values, no longer reach logs, error text or delegation audit details. The authorization callback logs the provider's error code the same way and no longer logs its description.
- **Hold custom metadata secrets to the tenant of the surface that sends them** — On a tenant surface, a $SECRET: reference in the Target's custom metadata, in either response custom metadata or in the shared Transit Point custom metadata resolves only a secret of the surface's own tenant, or an appliance-wide one in warn mode. Another tenant's secret and a new appliance-wide secret are refused on save with 400, and so is a reference to a secret that does not exist, whether new or already stored. A reassignment that would create such a reference answers 409, and the request path refuses them at runtime. A stored appliance-wide reference is kept only in warn mode and only on the destination that already sent it: the endpoint of its Target or Transit Point for request metadata and Target authentication, and the caller for response metadata, so moving a secret into response metadata is refused. Only enabled metadata counts as sent. A surface with no tenant is unchanged.
- **Refuse appliance-wide secrets in a tenant's MCP tool discovery** — POST /v1/mcp-proxies/discover-tools with a tenant token may now use only that tenant's own static secret. Before, it could load an appliance-wide secret and send its value to an endpoint the caller chose. Such a request is refused with 400 'Target auth secret is not accessible' whatever tenant_credential_isolation says, because discovery stores nothing that the stricter rule would break. Operator sessions and appliance-wide tokens are unchanged.
- **Hold a tenant A2A Proxy's Direct Line secret to the proxy's tenant** — Saving a tenant A2A Proxy that names another tenant's secret, or a new appliance-wide one, is refused with 400 'A2A proxy secret is not accessible'. Before, a tenant could send the appliance's Direct Line secret to a base URL it picked. An update that keeps the stored appliance-wide secret and base URL is saved with a warning in warn mode and refused in enforce mode, where a request through such a proxy also fails as target unavailable before the secret's value is used. A tenant reassignment that would give a tenant A2A Proxy an appliance-wide Direct Line secret answers 409, whether the proxy moves into a tenant or its secret becomes appliance-wide. An operator's appliance-wide A2A Proxy stays usable by tenant surfaces as a shared bot.
- **Refuse a tenant provider holding an outside secret on a new surface leg** — A tenant credential provider that sends the gateway's own credential and still holds a secret outside its tenant now follows the appliance-wide provider rule on a tenant surface. In warn mode it is kept only on the legs that already sent it at the same endpoint; on a new or redirected Target, Transit Point or outbound_credentials leg it is refused with 400; in enforce mode it is refused everywhere. This stops a token minted with the appliance's OAuth client from reaching an endpoint the tenant chose. Changing a provider's default_scopes now drops its kept secret references, and the startup credential policy report names surfaces that bind such a provider.
- **Stop logging every surface on the listener for a request to an unknown path** — A request to a path no surface serves, which needs no authentication, wrote one warning per surface on the listener, naming every tenant's surfaces and routes, so anyone reading the log learned them and any caller could flood the log. It now writes one warning, and that line and the two the gateway logs before routing carry the request path escaped and cut to 256 characters.
- **Stop Transit Points passing a caller's X-Forwarded headers to the target** — A Transit Point forwarded its caller's X-Forwarded-For, X-Forwarded-Host, X-Forwarded-Proto and Forwarded headers to its target as if the gateway had set them, so a target trusting the gateway as its proxy took the caller's claims about its address, host and scheme. The caller's Forwarded, X-Real-IP and X-Forwarded-* headers, also when spelled with underscores, are now dropped, over HTTP and Fabric.
- **Stop listeners with different IPv6 external URLs counting as one host** — An inbound listener passes /outbound/ requests on to an outbound listener whose external URLs share a host name with its own. The host was cut at the first colon, so IPv6 address URLs such as https://[2001:db8::1] and https://[2001:db8::2] looked like one host, and an inbound listener could expose another address's Transit Points. Hosts are now parsed as URLs and an IPv6 address matches only the same address.
- **Block cloud metadata and private IPv4 addresses inside 6to4 and NAT64 IPv6** — The SSRF checks on target, webhook, OAuth and JWKS URLs, and on the addresses their hosts resolve to, decoded only the IPv4-mapped form of an IPv6 address. A 6to4 (2002::/16) or NAT64 (64:ff9b::/96 or 64:ff9b:1::/96) address carrying a cloud metadata, loopback or RFC 1918 IPv4 address passed as a public one. Those forms are now checked as the IPv4 address they carry, and webhook URLs also refuse the NAT64 local-use prefix 64:ff9b:1::/48.
- **Make the presented API key's DID replace any body-asserted caller identity** — On a direct request to a surface whose inbound Identity is From API Key, the presented key's DID replaces any identity the request body asserts, so a key holder cannot choose another DID, and policies naming a presentation's DID stop matching. Unless the body asserts the key's own DID, its trust-registry claims (`provider_did`, `trust_registry_did`, `authority_did`, `identity_issuer_did`, `agent_dna` and trust verifications) are dropped, so Trust Check presets that read `input.agent.provider_did` deny. Gateway-plane policy runs before the key resolves and sees none of these. On a `fabric://` target only `access_point.inbound_policy` sees the key's DID. Fabric receive does not read this mode.
- **Verify STS subject tokens only with the client tenant's JWT strategies** — The token endpoint verified a JWT subject token, actor token or jwt-bearer assertion with the first JWT verification strategy of any tenant whose expected issuer matched, so a tenant could register another tenant's issuer with its own keys and have its tokens accepted for that issuer. It now uses the authenticated client's tenant's strategies for the issuer, or the appliance-global ones when the tenant has none, and an appliance-global client uses only appliance-global strategies. The jwt-bearer grant now authenticates the client before it verifies the assertion, so a bad client is refused with invalid_client before any assertion is checked.
- **Stop spoofed forwarded headers from steering the STS throttle and discovery** — The token endpoint throttle keyed its source on the first X-Forwarded-For entry and counted a client id across every source, so a caller could pick its own source and lock a known client id out everywhere. It now uses the client IP resolved through client_ip.trusted_proxies, counts an IPv6 address by its /64, and counts a client id separately for each source, so the per_client limit applies per client and source and no longer bounds guessing one client's secret from many sources; give each client a long random secret. STS discovery now reads X-Forwarded-Host and X-Forwarded-Proto, or Forwarded, only from a peer in client_ip.trusted_proxies when no inbound external_urls is set.
- **Refuse an Ed25519 JWK whose public key is not 32 bytes** — A JWT verification strategy's OKP key went to the EdDSA verifier without a length check, and a token whose header named EdDSA against a key with a short or empty x made the verifier panic, failing the request. The key must now decode to exactly 32 bytes, and a key whose crv names a curve other than Ed25519 is refused too, both as an invalid token.
- **⭐ Leave configured key and secret paths out of support exports** — The redacted `.atgx` storage export left out secret directories only by their default names, so a `vc_keys` directory configured under another name, holding the transit token signing key and issuer keys, was exported unencrypted inside the export. The export now leaves out the paths configured for `identities`, `secrets`, `vc_keys`, `passkeys`, `apikeys`, `certificates`, `sessions`, `avatars`, `delegation_vault` and `identity_hash_pepper`, matched by resolved path, and every `transit_token_key.json` file in either form by name. Treat earlier exports of a gateway with renamed storage paths as holding its keys.
- **Withhold agent headers named like a Transit Point's delegated credentials** — A Transit Point forwarded a managed agent header named like a delegated credential binding's `custom_header` whenever the gateway did not add the credential itself, as for a binding scoped to tools, so the agent could send the target a credential of its choosing under that name, or a second value beside the gateway's. Every value of such a header, compared without regard to case, is now withheld on every request.
- **Send the target one value of each Access Point credential header** — An Access Point and Fabric receive forwarded a caller header named like the Target's `target_auth` header, and added the gateway's `target_auth` and delegated credential headers beside any caller value, so the target could receive the caller's value first. A caller header named like the `target_auth` header is now never forwarded, including `Authorization` kept by `forward_source_auth_header`, and each credential header the gateway adds replaces every caller value. A caller that sent the target its own key under the `target_auth` header name must now rely on the gateway's.
- **Withhold `Set-Cookie2` and `Authentication-Info` from Transit Point responses** — A Transit Point already kept a target's `Set-Cookie` and `WWW-Authenticate` from the managed agent; it now also withholds `Set-Cookie2` and `Authentication-Info`, which carry the same kind of session and authentication state.

### Added

- **Bind an Access Point's Target to a provider of external consent** — A surface's outbound_credentials take consent_mode external with a subject, owner or caller, for its Access Point Target, whose grants the vault keeps by binding under the hop target, as a Transit Point's are kept under its id. The binding must be the only one in outbound_credentials, on a surface without Transit Points, and is held in one place, the surface's or one variant's. Its provider has an issuer and the surface's own tenant, its resource is permitted for its issuer, and the Target's endpoint is a public https URL with that resource's origin, and on an A2A or AP2 Access Point not the root of that origin, since the grant goes at or under the endpoint path there. The grant goes in a header, so neither inject_as meta, a target.auth credential of the Target's own, nor a forwarded caller JWT (forward_header) is accepted with it, and at-rest encryption and the identity hash pepper must be set. The surface sends the vendor no presentation of the agent, so an identity VP (inject_vp or identity_slots.inbound), an enabled workload binding and the surface's DID injected protocol_native are refused with it, and it charges callers nothing, so an enabled x402 or MPP payment policy is refused too: a payment is settled before the grant is released, which can still be refused. Enabled target.custom_metadata is refused as well, since it would send the vendor values of the gateway's beside the grant. A variant that keeps an owner binding keeps the surface's caller authentication, Access Point inbound_policy and trust_check_list and Target policy, since every caller they let through acts as the owner. The connect API, the grants report and removal take hop target; the Target's grants are removed and revoked when its binding goes or narrows, as a Transit Point's are, and the daily sweep keeps them only while it is served. A save warns of an enabled variant that keeps the binding under its own Access Point or Target settings, and a variant created from one that holds it does not copy it and is created disabled, with a warning, since it would send the copied Target requests without the grant. No agent-keyed path, legacy or 2026-07-28, ever serves such a binding. The Surface Builder's Credential Delegation element keeps such a binding, and its subject, when the surface is saved, shows it read-only, offering only its removal, which revokes its grants, and asks for an agent identity only beside a binding without external consent.
- **Send the Access Point's Target its grant in place of the caller's credentials** — An Access Point whose Target is bound by external consent sends the Target the owner's grant, or the identified caller's own, in a header that replaces any of the same name, and never the caller's credential. The request goes only to the origin of the provider's resource, through Strict egress pinned to the vetted addresses, with redirects refused and the destination checked again on every request, and only to the Target's endpoint path on MCP, or at or under it on A2A, with no query string, so a request with one is refused 400 rather than sent without it: a path the vendor might read otherwise than the gateway, with a dot segment plain or percent-encoded, a segment of dots and spaces alone, a byte that is not ASCII once decoded, an encoded separator, an empty segment, a path parameter or a second encoding, is refused 400, and a method the protocol does not send the grant with is refused 405. Of the caller's headers the Target gets only the protocol headers its requests need, Content-Type, Accept, MCP-Protocol-Version, Mcp-Session-Id, A2A-Version and A2A-Extensions (also as X-A2A-Extensions), so no cookies, credentials, x-caller-* or x-did-* header, method, path or host override, or trace id of the caller's; the gateway adds no caller-info or DID header, asks for an uncompressed answer and refuses a compressed one. A caller the Access Point did not identify is refused 403, so it never acts as the owner or as another caller; a public discovery read carries no grant and no query string. The grant is released, refreshed and answered by the same code as a Transit Point's: a grant due a refresh is refreshed first, single-flight; the Target's 401 whose challenge says invalid_token gets one refresh and one retry for bodies within 1 MiB, audited under the same caller and marked used, and a second 401 refuses that request and keeps the grant; a 403 for a missing scope answers scope_insufficient. Refusals carry the fixed reasons of a Transit Point's, connect_required with its hint, scope_insufficient and connector_unavailable, as a JSON-RPC error on an MCP Access Point and a problem JSON otherwise, no-store, and a refusal before anything is sent for the path, the method or a broken rule is audited request_rejected as target_path_refused, target_method_refused or connector_unavailable. A surface whose binding breaks a save rule is refused before any payment. The Target's cookies and authentication challenges never reach the caller, and the legacy SSE fallback is not used. A 2026-07-28 MCP request is refused with -32022, and a request that arrived over Fabric, a public discovery read included, is refused before any payment is asked for, since the caller a peer gateway asserts never keys a grant and a discovery read would carry the peer's headers to the vendor.
- **Accept a Google service account JSON key as a provider signing key** — The signing-key secret of an oauth2_jwt_bearer or oauth2_client_credentials_jwt_auth provider may now hold the whole service account key file, up to 16 KiB. The gateway signs with its private_key and sends its private_key_id as the kid header unless Key ID is set, so the downloaded file no longer needs to be taken apart.
- **⭐ Show the managed agent's card next to the served card in Surface Builder** — A2A and AP2 surfaces get an Agent Cards view that compares the managed agent's own card with the card the gateway serves, marks what the gateway changed, shows the fetch result and the Target Authentication used, and refreshes the card on demand.
- **Show the agent card fetch status on the Managed Agent panel** — The Managed Agent panel shows whether the gateway can fetch the agent's card and why not, with a link to the Agent Cards view.
- **Add agent card preview and refresh endpoints to the surface management API** — GET /api/v1/surfaces/{id}/agent-card (surfaces.view) and POST .../agent-card/refresh (surfaces.edit) report the card the base route serves beside the managed agent's own card, how the gateway's fetch went and whether Target Authentication was sent, as a 200 for every fetch result. The fetch is pinned, records no metric, is audited as agent_card.preview_fetch without URL, secret or card, and is limited to 10 a minute per user and per surface; past the limit a read answers with the last preview and a refresh gets 429. A failed fetch keeps showing the last good card of the same running surface for up to 10 minutes.
- **Let operators set the credential provider token request timeout** — The bootstrap [credential_policy] section accepts token_request_timeout_secs, from 1 to 120 seconds and 30 by default; 0 or a larger value fails startup. It bounds the token requests of service grants and their refreshes, the legacy delegated refresh, the legacy code exchange and the modern consent exchange; a grant's connect code exchange and refresh keep a fixed 30 seconds. A service token request that runs past it fails and starts the retry backoff. Changing it needs a restart.
- **Accept a bracketed IPv6 listener `bind_address` such as `[::1]`** — A listener's `bind_address` may now write an IPv6 address in brackets, such as `[::1]` or `[::]`, as it appears in URLs. The bracketed and bare forms name the same socket, so two listeners using both on one port are refused as duplicates.

### Changed

- **Require checked callers for every binding with external consent** — An external-consent binding needs callers that are authenticated by something the gateway can check. On a Transit Point as on the Access Point Target, a binding with consent_mode external is refused at save on a surface whose Access Point, in its base or any variant, has a did_auth method without allowed_dids, which accepts any DID that signs its challenge, or a jwt_bearer method without audiences while it publishes no protected_resource, which accepts a token its issuer minted for any other service; and a binding with subject caller is refused on a surface whose Access Point takes the gateway's resource access tokens (mcp_http.authorization), which name a caller by its subject without the identity provider that named it. A surface stored outside the API that breaks one of these rules answers connector_unavailable and reads no grant, and so does one saved through the API before these rules applied. Its grants are kept, not revoked, and none is released until the caller authentication is tightened (allowed_dids, audiences); removing the binding removes them and revokes them at the vendor.
- **Send no presentation of the agent through a Transit Point with external consent** — A Transit Point whose transit_credentials use consent_mode external is refused at save when it would send its vendor a presentation of the agent: an identity VP, from identity_injection.inject_vp or a managed_identity of its own, or an enabled workload_binding, as the Access Point Target already is. A Transit Point stored outside the API with one answers connector_unavailable and reads no grant.
- **⭐ Let agents serving partner requests call every Transit Point of their surface** — A managed agent serving a request a paired gateway sent over Fabric now gets a transit token for every Transit Point of the surface, as it does for a direct caller, so it can call onward with no extra setting. Before, it got one only for Transit Points that set allow_fabric_callers, which was off by default and could be set only through the admin API. After upgrading, a partner that reaches a surface can make its agent call all of that surface's Transit Points, with the credentials each sends, its static target_auth included, and delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers takes effect on a Transit Point that sets it without allow_fabric_callers. Partner reach is now decided per surface: expose to each remote gateway (Publishing tab) only the surfaces whose Transit Points it may drive, and keep a sensitive Transit Point on a separate surface no partner reaches. After upgrading, review each remote gateway's exposure (a peer migrated from a release before exposure modes can reach every surface) and any Transit Point policy that admits every verified or non-anonymous caller, which also admits a partner's call; see FABRIC.md, Which Transit Points a partner can drive.
- **Report providers and surfaces that break the credential rules at startup** — At startup and on every configuration reload the gateway logs one warning per credential provider or surface whose stored configuration breaks the https token endpoint or tenant credential isolation rule, with its id, tenant and the rule, so operators can fix them before setting the rules to enforce.
- **Offer tenant surfaces only their own providers and secrets in the dashboard** — The Surface Builder and the credential provider editor list only the credential providers and secrets of the surface's or provider's own tenant, plus the appliance-wide authorization code providers a tenant surface may keep using. A stored choice outside that tenant stays visible: an appliance-wide one is labelled as stopping to work once tenant isolation is enforced, and one of another tenant as already refused by the gateway. The Token Endpoint field shows an inline error for an http URL and points to the bootstrap credential_policy.insecure_token_endpoints list.
- **Add audit detail values and shorter token failure texts for grants** — Delegation audit details gain the values insecure_token_endpoint, appliance_wide_credential and the _stale suffix on a grant, and a refused token request, refresh, code exchange or SAML Graph API token request now logs, returns and audits only the OAuth error code, so consumers that parse audit details or token failure texts must accept the new values and the shorter texts.
- **Label a Transit Point's managed identity resolve metrics with the surface name** — A Transit Point's outbound request labelled `agent_gateway_managed_identity_resolve_total` and its duration histogram with the surface ID, while every other path used the surface name. It now reports the surface name, so one surface counts under one `channel_id`. Update alerts and dashboards that filter these metrics by a Transit Point surface's ID.
- **Count callers behind an unlisted load balancer as one STS throttle source** — The STS token endpoint throttle, on by default, now takes the caller's address from the connection, and from X-Forwarded-For only when the connecting peer is in client_ip.trusted_proxies, which is empty by default. Behind a load balancer that is not listed there, every caller it forwards shares one source, so a burst of failed client logins through it locks every STS client out for lockout_secs. List the balancer in client_ip.trusted_proxies; a lockout of a caller no trusted proxy forwarded logs a warning saying so.
- **Forward a `HEAD` of a Transit Point discovery document without credentials** — A `HEAD` of a discovery document, such as an agent card, through a Transit Point needed a transit token and, on a Transit Point with credential bindings, carried the delegated credential of the caller the token named. It is now handled like a `GET`: no transit token is checked and it is forwarded without the delegated credential, as it already was without the `target_auth` one. A target that answered such a `HEAD` only with the credential now answers it as it answers an anonymous `GET`. Which paths count as discovery documents is described in `docs/SOURCE_AUTH.md`.
- **Hold a variant's own DID Auth session TTL to the 24-hour cap** — A variant whose Access Point overrides its caller authentication mints sessions with its own first method, so a session_ttl_seconds above 86400 there is now refused on a save that sets it and named in a warning at startup, as the surface's own already was. A save that keeps a stored value is accepted, and a DID Auth method that is not the first, which mints no session, is not bounded.

### Fixed

- **Refuse a malformed caller principal in the grants report** — GET /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants now answers 400 invalid_principal for a principal that is neither owner nor an identity hash, as starting a connect and removing grants already did, instead of reporting that caller as not connected.
- **Stop dashboard log updates from reading the whole log file into memory** — A dashboard tab that asked for log entries after an old position, for example after sitting in the background or reconnecting after a restart, made the gateway parse every line from that position to the end of the log file before keeping only the newest entries. On a large log this spiked memory by hundreds of MB and could get the container killed for running out of memory. The gateway now finds the newest lines by scanning back from the end of the file and reads only those, and never more than 8 MiB, however old the position is.
- **Show the full number of newest log lines in the dashboard when lines are long** — The log view's first page and its updates guessed where the newest lines began by assuming 512 bytes per line, so logs with longer lines showed fewer lines than the view asks for. The gateway now counts line breaks back from the end of the file and returns the full number of newest lines, up to 8 MiB of log.
- **Stop the dashboard log view showing a line twice after it loads** — When the gateway read the end of the log file and the read started inside a multi-byte character, such as the `…` that marks a clipped value, the positions it gave each log line were too small. The next update then sent the newest line again under its real position, so the log view showed it twice and could bring back lines hidden by Clear. Positions are now the real byte offsets of the lines.
- **Stop reusing a service token after its provider or a secret it sends changes** — Service tokens for client credentials, JWT bearer and private_key_jwt providers are now cached under a key that covers the provider's settings, the sorted scopes and the id, tenant and updated_at of each secret the provider's type sends, read by id. Editing a provider, rotating one of those secrets or moving one to another tenant requests a new token instead of reusing the old one, including on legacy bindings, which reused one token per provider before. A deleted secret the type sends, a secrets store error or an enforced tenant rule stops a cached token at once. A reference left from another type, such as an API key kept after a provider became client credentials, is neither read nor part of the key, so deleting or rotating its secret no longer fails requests or renews the token. Expired service tokens of the same provider are removed from the vault in the same step that stores a new one, which reads the vault once. Upgrade note: each binding and target authentication requests one new token after the upgrade.
- **Send one token request per credential at a time** — Requests that need the same service token, for the same surface, provider, scopes and secret versions, now wait for one token request on each replica and reuse its token from the vault, instead of each sending its own request to the credential provider when the cache is empty or expired.
- **Back off token requests to a failing credential provider for up to 60 s** — After a service token request fails or returns other scopes than requested, the next request for the same surface, provider, scopes and secret versions waits 1 s from the end of the failed request, doubling to 60 s, and callers meanwhile fail at once without contacting the provider. A success clears the wait. Missing secrets, store errors and refused token endpoints never start one, and a secret that cannot be read is now reported as unavailable rather than as a failed token request.
- **Keep using a valid service token when its renewal fails** — A cached service token is now renewed half its lifetime before it expires, at most 5 minutes and at least 60 seconds early, while other requests keep using it. When the renewal fails at the token endpoint or is held back by the retry backoff, the cached token is still injected, marked stale, until 60 seconds before it expires, so a short outage of the credential provider no longer fails proxied requests. A changed provider, a missing secret, a secrets store error or a refused token endpoint never serves the stale token. Legacy delegation audit details gain the value <grant>_stale, for example client_credentials_stale.
- **Keep local token grant failures out of the token backoff** — A malformed or missing signing key, a missing token endpoint or client, or another unusable provider setting no longer starts the token retry backoff or serves a cached token as stale; only failures caused by the token endpoint do. The API status and audit text are unchanged.
- **Warn once per token grant about an out-of-tenant secret** — In warn mode the out-of-tenant secret warning is now logged once when a token is granted instead of on every cache hit and twice on a fresh grant; the tenant check is one shared predicate.
- **Fail startup on a malformed insecure token endpoint entry** — credential_policy.insecure_token_endpoints entries that do not parse, are not http, or carry a user name or password now fail startup with the entry named, instead of being silently ignored.
- **Report every provider secret that sits outside the provider tenant** — The credential policy report now flags a tenant provider holding another tenant's secret and an appliance-wide provider holding a tenant secret, not only a tenant provider holding an appliance-wide secret. It counts every secret reference a provider stores, including one its type does not send.
- **Report surfaces the credential policy check could not inspect** — A surface whose references cannot be collected now logs a warning naming its id, tenant and error, and the clean line says how many surfaces could not be inspected instead of claiming nothing breaks a rule.
- **Tell a refused agent card fetch the real wait** — The agent card preview limit is a burst of 10 then one fetch every 6 seconds; a 429 now carries a Retry-After of 1 to 6 seconds instead of a fixed 60, and the docs and dashboard toast say so.
- **Warn instead of erroring on an http token endpoint in the dashboard** — The credential provider editor now shows a warning, not an invalid-field error, for an http token endpoint, explaining that new or changed ones need an exact entry in credential_policy.insecure_token_endpoints and that an unchanged stored one is still accepted in warn mode.
- **Report a preview target that does not resolve as unreachable** — An agent card preview of a host that does not resolve now reports unreachable (502) instead of blocked_by_egress (403), as the served card route does when it fetches without Target authentication. A served card fetched with Target authentication and the proxy forward still answer 403 blocked by egress for such a host. The event hook and mediator directory reasons, JWKS fetch errors and x402 facilitator construction errors now name a failed host lookup as such.
- **Read credential provider secrets by id instead of listing the secrets store** — A service token grant and an API key read now look up each secret they send by its secret id instead of listing the secrets store. The filesystem store reads every secret file only to rebuild its id index when asked for an id it does not know, such as a deleted secret or one another replica wrote. The tenant check, its warning and the errors are unchanged.
- **Keep a service token request's slot while a caller holds it** — The per-key single-flight and retry backoff slots are released only when no caller holds them and no failure is remembered. With many keys in use, a second request for the same key therefore waits for the one in flight instead of sending its own. A failure count is forgotten ten minutes after its retry time.
- **Let the access token form name the delegation vault in a resource pattern** — The access token form refused a resource pattern naming `delegation-vault` with "Every resource selector must start with a known resource kind followed by ':'", although the backend accepts it. The dashboard kept its own list of resource kinds, which did not include the delegation vault, so a token that confines the connect API and grants to a tenant's surfaces (`delegation-vault:<surface id>`) could not be made from the form. The list now matches the backend's, and a backend test fails while a resource kind is missing from it.
- **⭐ Identify each caller by the API key it presents** — An inbound Identity set to From API Key gave every caller the DID of one stored key. The caller DID now comes from the API key each request presents: one DID per API Key Provider key, reported to policy as `source_auth`, or one per plain API Key secret, shared by its holders and reported as `unverified`; a secret whose id equals a provider key id shares that key's DID. A failed or revoked key resolves no caller identity. A stored inbound key id is ignored and dropped on save. A key that is also a Managed Agent's key gives its callers that Managed Agent's DID; a surface policy can refuse them by `input.source_auth.key_name`.
- **Stop asking for an API key on the inbound Identity in the Surface Builder** — The inbound Identity's From API Key option shows "Each caller is identified by the API key it presents." instead of a key picker, and Save never sends an inbound key id. Save is blocked unless the first Caller Context method is API Key or API Key Provider. The protected and external Identities keep the key picker.
- **Give concurrent first requests for one identity the same DID** — Concurrent first requests for an identity with no record could each mint a different DID. The identity record is now created only if none exists for its hash, under a lock on that record, so every concurrent first request gets the one stored DID.
- **Title-case Identity Binding events in the Audit Log** — The Audit Log showed `identity_binding_verified` and `identity_binding_failed` records as lowercase raw names. They now show as Identity Binding Verified and Identity Binding Failed, like the other events.
- **Check every unfingerprinted delegated grant against the identity pepper** — With no pepper fingerprint recorded, the vault took the grants stored for its own as soon as one had the id its identity hash pepper derives, so grants written under another pepper beside it went unnoticed. Every grant is now read: one of another pepper makes them all a mismatch, kept in memory until a grant is removed so a purge takes effect at once, and grants all of this pepper get its fingerprint recorded, so later checks read the fingerprint alone. When none can be read, the vault reports the unreadable grant as unavailable rather than as a pepper mismatch.
- **Send non-ASCII credential header values to a Transit Point's HTTP target** — A Transit Point dropped a `target_auth` or delegated credential header whose value was not ASCII, so an HTTP target received the request without the credential. Such a value now reaches an HTTP target byte for byte. A Fabric target can carry only text values, so a Transit Point now refuses a Fabric request whose `target_auth` header cannot be carried with `502`, and one whose delegated credential header cannot be carried with `503`, instead of forwarding it without the credential.
- **Answer MCP Transit Point credential failures with a JSON-RPC error** — An MCP Transit Point whose `target_auth` credential cannot be resolved or sent, or whose custom metadata cannot be injected, now answers with a JSON-RPC error (`-32603`) carrying the request's id, with the same `502` or `500` status, instead of a problem JSON body that an MCP client cannot match to its request.
- **Answer a caller an MCP Transit Point refuses with a JSON-RPC error** — An MCP Transit Point whose `source_auth` refuses the caller's credential now answers with a JSON-RPC error (`-32001`, `Caller credential missing or invalid`, id `null`) with the same `401` status, instead of a problem JSON body an MCP client cannot read as a JSON-RPC response. Other Transit Points still answer with problem JSON.
- **Record unreachable and timed-out Transit Point calls as failed** — A Transit Point call whose target could not be reached, did not answer in time, or answered with a response the gateway could not read or process, such as an invalid `2026-07-28` MCP response, was not recorded in the dashboard's traffic, so a failing target looked idle. These calls are now recorded as failed.
- **Never serve a deleted or disabled surface's Transit Points after a reload** — A reload placed every surface that was not disabled on its outbound listener, a deleted one included, and returned before a rebuilt outbound listener had registered its surfaces, so a surface deleted right after the reload was not retired there and its Transit Points stayed in service. Only active surfaces are now served, and a reload returns once each rebuilt outbound listener has registered its surfaces.
- **Refuse requests to a disabled or deleted surface on a kept-alive connection** — A connection kept alive on a TLS inbound listener across a reload kept serving the Access Point of a surface that had since been disabled or deleted, from the copy it held. Such a request is now refused with `404`, as on a new connection.
- **Evict a disabled surface's policies, tool gating, limiters and circuit breaker** — Disabling a surface left its compiled policies, MCP tool gating and rate limiters in memory, and deleting one left its circuit breaker, so a surface enabled again or created under the same id started from the old state. Disabling or deleting a surface now evicts all of them, its variants' and Transit Points' included.
- **Fail instead of keeping two transit token keys when encryption settings differ** — Two instances sharing `[storage_paths] vc_keys` that disagree on encryption at rest could each create a transit token key at the same time, one plaintext and one encrypted, and then refuse each other's tokens. The instance that finds the other form beside the file it created now removes its own and fails to start with an error saying so. The directory is also synced after a secret file is created, so a crash cannot lose the new key after it was used.
- **Report IPv4 callers of a dual-stack listener in IPv4 form** — An IPv4 caller of a listener bound to `::` on a dual-stack host, and an IPv4-mapped address in `X-Forwarded-For`, were recorded as `::ffff:a.b.c.d`. The resolved client IP used by the login limits and request records is now `a.b.c.d`.
- **Keep the `transit.source_auth` refusal under every surface variant** — A complete variant whose shared transit settings left `source_auth` out served the Transit Points of a surface that sets `transit.source_auth`, which every other route refuses with `503`; it is now refused too. Loading a surface whose variant sets `transit.shared.source_auth` now logs the same warning as one set on the surface.
- **Bind only configured listener addresses, whatever their address family** — A port that no inbound listener names is no longer bound on `0.0.0.0` and is reported as an error instead. A bind the operating system reports as in use is skipped only when a listener already bound on the port accepts its connections, where an IPv6 wildcard covers IPv4 only on a dual-stack socket, so an address that is not served is no longer skipped silently.
- **Audit a Fabric request refused by a Target bound by external consent** — A request that arrives over Fabric for a surface whose Access Point Target is bound by external consent is still refused 403, and is now recorded as a request_rejected at admission, consent_identity_required, so these refusals appear in the Audit Log.
- **Offer no second credential beside an external consent binding** — The Surface Builder's Credential Delegation element disables Add credential while it holds a binding with external consent, which must be the Access Point Target's only binding, and says why, instead of letting a second binding fail on save. It no longer shows the gateway's callback URL for such a binding, whose grant arrives through the connect API's own redirect.

### Removed

- **Stop reading a Transit Point's allow_fabric_callers setting** — allow_fabric_callers no longer has an effect, since a token issued for a partner's request now opens every Transit Point of the surface. A stored surface that has it still loads, and the next save drops it.

## [1.0.4] - 2026-11-02

### Breaking

- **Refuse to delete a credential provider that a surface still binds** — Deleting a credential provider that a surface binds, in its base or a variant, through `outbound_credentials` or a Transit Point's `transit_credentials`, or uses for the `credential_provider` target authentication of its Target or a Transit Point, now answers 409 `provider_in_use` instead of 204, which left every later call through the binding refused; clients that delete providers must remove the bindings first. A provider delete is serialised with surface writes, so no write binds the provider while it is being deleted. The dashboard shows the reason and the surface. Only surfaces that may reference the provider are considered, and the answer names the surface when the caller may see it. Deleting a provider with an issuer deletes each of its grants and the provider, then revokes each grant at the authorization server's RFC 7009 revocation endpoint, the refresh token and then the access token, at most eight grants at a time, each within 30 seconds, through pinned Strict `https` egress, with the client authenticating as its `token_endpoint_auth_method` says. The revocations run after the delete answers and after the provider lock is released, so a slow vendor holds up no surface write. Revocation is best effort and audited as `token_revoked` with its outcome and the caller that deleted the provider; the grant is deleted whatever the outcome. Only that provider's grants are read: the provider is kept, with 503 naming the grant, while one of them cannot be read, for example because at-rest encryption is off, and another provider's unreadable grant never blocks the delete.
- **Connect the vendor calls of external consent directly, whatever proxy is set** — Reading an external consent provider's authorization server metadata, revoking its grants, and forwarding a request that carries a grant by binding now ignore `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` and the system proxy and connect directly, since a proxy would resolve the vendor's host again and connect to an address nobody vetted. On a network whose egress goes only through a proxy, allow direct egress to those vendors, or external consent fails to connect; the gateway warns at startup when a proxy variable is set. Every other outbound call uses a configured proxy as before.
- **Refuse to move a bound credential provider to another tenant** — `PUT /v1/tenant-ownership/credential-providers/{id}` now answers 409 for a provider that any surface binds, of any tenant, in its base or a variant, through `outbound_credentials` or a Transit Point's `transit_credentials`, or uses for target authentication, instead of moving it: its bindings would name another tenant's provider, and moving it back would serve their grants again. Remove the bindings first. A move is serialised with surface writes, and a write that binds a provider moved to a tenant its surface may not reference since the write checked its references is refused, as one that binds a deleted provider is.
- **Require a verified caller for credentials on Fabric receive** — A partner's legacy request to a Target with delegated credential bindings was keyed by the caller identity the partner named. Now a service-level binding (`api_key` and client credentials providers) refuses the request with `403` unless the receiving surface's own caller authentication verified a caller on it, such as the key the partner's `target_auth` sends, and a per-user (`oauth2_authorization_code`) binding always refuses it. Partners that relied on the identity they named, or on consent through the partner, stop getting credentials: give the receiving surface caller authentication the partner's `target_auth` satisfies for service-level bindings, and route per-user calls through a Transit Point that sets `delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers`, on a surface whose caller authentication tells a partner's users apart. Agent card and discovery reads are not affected.
- **⭐ Create surfaces with closed Access Points by default** — The admin API and dashboard now create surfaces with `access_point.public: false`, so an Access Point no longer admits anonymous callers: a missing or invalid credential is refused with `401`. A closed Access Point without source authentication (a `caller_authentication` method, or on MCP `mcp_http.authorization`) is saved, with `access_point_status: not_ready` and a `Warning` header, but answers every request, discovery reads included, with `503` until source authentication is added or `public` is set to `true`. The same applies after a PUT, PATCH, variant change or promote that leaves a closed surface, or one of its enabled variants, without source authentication. API and CLI clients that create surfaces must add source authentication or send `access_point.public: true`, and should check `access_point_status` or the `Warning` headers of the response. Surfaces stored before this release, built from DynamoDB, or restored without `public` keep their behaviour.
- **Older releases skip every surface created after this upgrade** — A release before this one rejects `access_point.public` and `caller_authentication.on_failure`, so after a rollback it skips, with a logged error, every surface that stores either one: every surface created after the upgrade, and every surface closed or opened since. Their routes answer `404` and they leave the dashboard and Fabric discovery until the upgrade is reapplied; the records stay on disk. Removing the fields to load them on the older release opens them to callers without credentials. Back up `_storage/agent_surfaces/` before upgrading.
- **⭐ Refuse a Transit Point that authenticates no caller unless it is marked open** — A Transit Point with `require_transit_token: false` and neither `mcp_http.authorization` nor `source_auth` served anyone who reached it, through this gateway and with its `target_auth` credential. Such a Transit Point must now be marked `allow_unauthenticated_callers: true`. The admin API refuses one that is not, on the surface or in a variant, with `400`, and also refuses `allow_unauthenticated_callers` on a Transit Point that authenticates its callers, `source_auth` beside `mcp_http.authorization`, and a `source_auth` a Transit Point does not support (`did_auth`, a `jwt_bearer` that forwards the token or names no audience, or a key read from anything but a header).
