A stored surface with such a Transit Point still loads, with a warning in the log, but that Transit Point refuses every request with `503` until it is fixed. A save that leaves such a stored Transit Point exactly as it is is still accepted, so a dashboard save of the surface works; one that changes it, or adds another, is refused. After upgrading, set `allow_unauthenticated_callers: true` on every Transit Point that should stay open, or give it `source_auth`, with an admin API `PUT` of the surface or in the dashboard; do not set the flag before upgrading, because a release before this one drops it when it saves the surface.
- **⭐ Mark surfaces whose Transit Points use source_auth so older releases skip them** — A release before this one ignores a Transit Point's `source_auth`. After a rollback, or on an older node sharing storage during a rolling upgrade or in an Active/Standby pair, it would serve such a Transit Point to anyone who reaches it, with its `target_auth` credential, when the transit token is off. A surface with a Transit Point that uses `source_auth`, on the base or in a variant, is now stored with `transit_point_source_auth: true`, which a release before this one refuses, so it skips the whole surface instead of serving it open. Upgrade every node that shares storage before giving a Transit Point `source_auth`, and do not roll back below this release while one uses it: first remove `source_auth` from each such Transit Point and turn its transit token back on, or delete the surface, then check no surface carries `transit_point_source_auth`. A Surface Template's surface is marked the same way, so an older release's admin API refuses a surface created from it; the dashboard rebuilds a template's surface without the mark, so remove `source_auth` from such templates too before rolling back, or back them up before upgrading.
- **Key a caller by how it was identified, not by its name alone** — The caller's identity hash, which keys delegated credentials, travels in the transit token as `user_identity_hash` and is in Transit Point policy input as `input.transit.caller.user_identity_hash`, was the SHA-256 of the bare principal, so an API key named `admin` and a JWT whose `sub` is `admin`, from any issuer, were one caller. It is now the SHA-256 of the authentication method, its issuer (the JWT `iss`, the API Key Provider agent, or the certificate an mTLS client certificate was verified against), the surface's tenant and the principal. The Workload Binding `user_hash` for `caller_source: did` follows the same rule. A JWT without an `iss` or a `sub` has no identity hash, so an Access Point that would give its caller a delegated credential answers it with `403`. Every delegated credential stored before this release is keyed by the old hash and is no longer found, so users who connected an account connect it again, and policies that compare `user_identity_hash` need the new value. The old entries stay in the delegation vault, with their refresh tokens, until an operator revokes them; see "Removing grants keyed by the old identity hash" in `docs/CREDENTIAL_DELEGATION.md`.
- **Refuse transit_token_mode reference in the admin API** — A surface or surface template that sets `transit.transit_token_mode: reference`, on the surface or in a variant's overrides, now gets a 400 on create, update and import, where it was accepted although the gateway always issued embedded tokens. Send `embedded` or leave the field out. A stored `reference` is rewritten to `embedded` on load.
- **Ignore log redaction set in config.toml** — With the local configuration source and no logging block in gateway.json, the gateway used to apply a [logging.redaction] table from config.toml. That table is now ignored, because earlier bootstrap generation wrote it with enabled = false, and the built-in rules apply instead; startup logs a warning when the table is present. To keep custom rules, move them to rules in gateway.json logging.redaction before upgrading. Rules set there replace the built-in rules, so also copy any built-in rules you still want from config/examples/gateway.example.json. See docs/CONFIGURATION_REFERENCE.md.
- **⭐ Refuse requests whose surface variant can't be resolved on every path** — A request that names a variant the surface doesn't have (including on a surface with no variants), names a disabled variant, has a disabled default variant, or reaches a surface whose default_variant_id names no variant is now refused on the Access Point, the Transit Point and Fabric receive. It is no longer served by the base surface or the default variant. An unknown or disabled variant gets 404, a misconfigured default 500, for legacy and modern requests alike, in place of the earlier 400, 503 and base-surface fallback. Each refusal is recorded as request_rejected at stage routing with reason unknown_variant, variant_disabled or the new misconfigured_default; an unknown_variant record carries the alias the URL named, cut to 32 characters, in requested_variant. On the Access Point and the Transit Point, a modern MCP request of any method (told by its MCP headers) is refused with a JSON-RPC error with code -32603 that echoes the request's id, recorded with that rpc_error_code; the body is read only to recover the id, within 16 KiB (or the endpoint's smaller max_request_bytes) and 2 seconds since the read runs before authentication, and one that can't be read within those, or carries no id, gets the error without one. Legacy MCP and other requests get a plain error.
- **Rename variant_alias to surface_variant on metrics and PayloadCaptured** — Connection metrics and the PayloadCaptured WebSocket update name the variant surface_variant instead of variant_alias, and take it from the request, so it matches the request's audit records. The default variant now shows its alias and a surface with no variants shows _base_, where both were empty, so a per-variant series steps at the upgrade for one metrics retention window (6 hours by default). Stored metrics written with variant_alias still load.
- **Name the variant of a policy change surface_variant, _base_ for base slots** — Policy change records name the variant whose slot was written surface_variant, the name every other record uses, instead of variant, and record _base_ for a slot on the base surface, which also applies to the variants that inherit it. Readers of policy change records, such as audit integrations and log consumers, must switch from variant to surface_variant. Records stored with variant still read, as surface_variant, and the Audit Log shows their variant.
- **⭐ Send a peer gateway only the variant alias its fabric:// Target pins** — A request over Fabric no longer carries the sending gateway's URL alias: a request to /route$beta on the sending gateway used to ask the peer for its own beta variant, and is now served by the peer's default variant or base surface, as an alias-less request is. Each gateway picks its own variant. To select a peer variant, pin it in the sending route's Target, fabric://<gateway>/<surface>$<alias>, or in a variant's Target override; this works for Access Point Targets and Transit Points, over a buffered ForwardRequest and a framed stream alike (a pinned alias on a ForwardRequest used to reach the peer as part of the surface id, which it couldn't find). The peer refuses a pinned alias it doesn't have, or has disabled, with 404. Setups that relied on matching aliases on both gateways must pin the alias in the Target.
- **Leave http_status off request_rejected records written on Fabric receive** — request_rejected records http_status only on HTTP refusals, at the Access Point and the Transit Point. A refusal on Fabric receive (via_fabric true, a stream or a ForwardRequest) involves no HTTP response and no longer records one. Readers that expected the field on every request_rejected record must treat it as optional; stored records keep theirs.

### Security

- **Never sweep grants while the identity hash pepper is unset or changed** — The daily sweep took every grant whose id differs from the one the gateway derives for grants of an earlier pepper, so a node started without AG_IDENTITY_HASH_PEPPER, or with the wrong one, deleted every grant and revoked its tokens at the vendors ten minutes after start, in a shared vault for every node. While the pepper is unset, or grants written under another are stored, the sweep now removes no grant and logs a warning. That holds when the pepper fingerprint file is lost too: unless a stored grant the gateway can read has the id its pepper derives, the grants count as written under another pepper, so the gateway neither sweeps, stores nor releases them, and never records its own fingerprint over them, which would have let a later sweep remove them all.
- **Key a partner's delegated credentials only by the caller this gateway verified** — On Fabric receive, the receiving gateway keyed its Target's delegated credentials by the caller identity the peer's request named, a payload pseudonym, a DID or a presentation's DID, so a paired gateway could name another caller and have that caller's stored grant sent to the Target. A partner's claim about who it acts for no longer selects stored credentials. A per-user (`oauth2_authorization_code`) binding refuses a legacy Fabric request with `403` and `delegation_not_enabled_for_partners`, since the caller a receiving surface verifies is normally the partner's own `target_auth` credential, which names all of its users alike; a partner's calls use a person's grant only through a Transit Point that opts in. A service-level binding needs a caller the surface verified, as on a direct call, else `403` and `partner_caller_not_verified`. Consent is never started for a legacy Fabric request, whose authorization URL would travel through the partner. Each refusal is a JSON-RPC error (`-32001`) or a problem JSON naming only that reason, recorded as a `request_rejected` audit event at `admission`. An agent card or discovery read is served as before. On a `2026-07-28` MCP request the agent half of the key is a caller DID only a connection issuer's presentation proved.
- **Name a partner's caller in a response presentation only when it was proved** — The presentation a receiving gateway signs for its agent's response to a Fabric request, when the Target has a Workload Binding, named whatever identity the peer's request carried as the user the agent acted for (`userIdentity`, `delegated: true`): a payload pseudonym, a DID computed from fields the peer sent, or a DID the peer only named. It now names the caller only when a presentation from one of the connection's issuers proved its DID, the same caller a transit token issued for the request carries, so the gateway never attests a delegation the peer only claimed.
- **Pass caller-info and DID identity headers only as the gateway sets them** — The Access Point and Fabric receive pass a managed agent `x-caller-did`, `x-caller-identity-source`, `x-caller-dna-uai`, `x-gateway-did`, `X-DID-Identity` and `X-DID-Signed-Identity` only as the gateway sets them, so the agent can rely on them to know who is calling, and a Transit Point sends its target none of them. A request that carries its own copy of one of these headers is forwarded without it.
- **Key delegated credentials by how the caller was identified** — The caller's identity hash, which keys delegated credentials and travels in the transit token as `user_identity_hash`, is now the SHA-256 of the authentication method, its issuer, the surface's tenant and the principal. The Workload Binding `user_hash` for `caller_source: did` follows the same rule. A JWT without a `sub` has no identity hash, so an Access Point or Transit Point that would give its caller a delegated credential answers it with `403`. Users who connected an account before this release connect it again, and policies that compare `user_identity_hash` need the new value. The delegated credentials stored before the upgrade are no longer used but keep their tokens until removed: revoke them through the delegation vault API or the dashboard's Credential Tokens tab, as `docs/CREDENTIAL_DELEGATION.md` describes.
- **Let operators cap how long transit tokens live, 300 seconds by default** — Whoever holds a transit token can call its surface's Transit Points as the caller, with the credentials they inject, until it expires, so a longer lifetime widens the window in which a leaked token can be replayed. A surface writer can lengthen it only up to the appliance's `transit_token_max_lifetime_secs`, 300 seconds unless an operator raises it, to at most 900. Upgrade every instance that shares surface storage, a Standby included, before raising the ceiling above 300 or setting any surface's lifetime: an earlier release refuses tokens living longer than 300 seconds, so its requests get an intermittent 401, and drops a stored lifetime the next time it saves the surface, which sets the lifetime back to 300 seconds.
- **Refuse transit_token_mode reference, which exposed caller context** — The gateway has no server-side store for transit tokens, so a surface that chose `transit.transit_token_mode: reference` was exposing its `caller_context_fields` to its agent while expecting them kept server-side. The admin API now refuses `reference`, on a surface or in a variant, with a 400. A surface stored with `reference` is rewritten to `embedded` when it loads, with a warning naming the surface and variant, so later writes are not refused.
- **⭐ Stop logging the startup configuration and the secrets it holds** — On every start the gateway logged its whole bootstrap and gateway configuration at info level, including backup encryption keys, MPP and Stripe secret keys and x402 facilitator private keys whenever they were configured as values instead of env:// references. Startup now logs one summary line (configuration source, server and auth mode, secrets backend, redaction state and surface counts), and these settings print as <redacted> wherever the configuration is formatted. Rotate every secret that was configured as a value, because logs from earlier versions contain it.
- **⭐ Log session tokens and DID Auth session ids as an 8-character prefix** — Dashboard session tokens and DID Auth session ids were written in full by storage log lines and errors on every save, delete and start, when a DID Auth session was issued, when a session delete failed, and by the local encryption trace, and a request with a personal access token logged part of the token. A session token now appears only as its first 8 characters, access tokens are no longer looked up as dashboard sessions, storage parse errors give the error kind and position instead of the record content, and the encryption check, encryption migration and backups name only the directory of a file they cannot read. If logs from earlier versions were kept or shared, end all dashboard and DID Auth sessions after upgrading.
- **Redact query values, URLs and MCP session ids in logs and traces** — The access log and the http.request span's http.uri attribute held full query strings, including OAuth codes and tokens sent as query parameters, and the http.url span attribute and the forwarding, endpoint, mirror and facilitator logs held full Target URLs, including credentials and query keys. Query values now become <redacted> with parameter names kept, URLs lose credentials and query values, transport errors are logged without the URL, RPC endpoints are logged by scheme, host and port only, and MCP session ids appear only as their first 8 characters. Rotate any provider key embedded in an RPC, Target or mirror URL, and purge or truncate log files and OTLP trace data kept from earlier versions.
- **Stop returning Target URLs and transport errors in gateway error responses** — 502 and 503 responses for an unreachable Target, a failed agent card fetch or a failed synchronous Fabric mirror included the configured Target or mirror URL and the HTTP client error text, so any caller could read endpoint URLs and any keys in them, and a gateway that failed to forward a Fabric request sent its Target URL and error details back to the calling gateway, which returned them to its caller. A failed MCP Proxy tool call returned the REST request URL, including query keys, the argument values and the backend's reply to the MCP client, and wrote the same text to the log and the dashboard. These responses now name only the kind of failure, such as the HTTP status or network error, and the detail stays in the server log without the URL.
- **Stop logging policy input, request headers and request bodies** — Every policy-checked request logged the whole policy input at info level ([OPA-INPUT]), including the caller's message, MCP tool arguments, JWT claims and forwarded headers. The /onboard/ endpoints, which need no sign-in, logged every request header and body and listed every live onboarding session when given an unknown id. The Legacy SSE fallback logged the MCP request after delegated credentials and the identity VP were added to it, and the upstream's reply, and MCP clients could write any notifications/message data to the gateway log at a level they chose. These lines now log ids, status codes, sizes and decisions only, REST tool failures are logged by kind, and onboarding sessions appear as an 8-character prefix, in the access log too. Revoke delegated credentials that may have been logged, and purge or truncate log files and OTLP log and trace data kept from earlier versions.
- **Stop logging payment signatures, authorizations and mandates** — PAYMENT-SIGNATURE values, signed x402 authorizations and their signature components, facilitator requests and responses, AP2 mandates and KB-JWT transaction data were logged at info level, and the embedded facilitator recorded each whole verify and settle request on its trace span. These values are no longer logged, and the facilitator spans record the payment scheme and network instead of the request.
- **Stop logging DIDComm message content and decrypted requests** — Basic message text, problem report comments and decrypted forward requests, which include the credentials the gateway adds for the Target, were logged. These lines now log the message type, id and size.
- **Log user ids instead of usernames, email addresses and token subjects** — Sign-in, session, auth check, WebSocket, user management, Microsoft Graph avatar and source authentication log lines named the user by username, which in SAML mode is the email address, or by the token's subject, email integrations logged their recipients, and dashboard WebSocket frames were logged as text. They now log the user id, a count or a length. OAuth token endpoint errors are logged and reported by their error and error_description fields instead of the whole response body.
- **Apply log redaction to OpenTelemetry log export** — Log records exported over OTLP never passed through the redaction rules, unlike the console, the log file and exported traces. Their bodies and attributes are now redacted, and the new opentelemetry.logs.targets setting in metrics.json limits which log targets are exported, by exact target or module path prefix; the default, "*", keeps exporting every target. If OTLP log or trace export was enabled, purge records from earlier versions in those backends.
- **Stop the dashboard writing DIDs and policy settings to the browser console** — The identity policy dialog and other dashboard pages logged identity DIDs, policy settings, permission values, version details and WebSocket update summaries to the browser console. These console messages are removed.
- **Log a refused variant alias cut to 32 characters, never as the caller sent it** — The warning written when a request's surface variant can't be resolved names the reason, the requested alias cut to 32 characters as the audit record holds it, on a disabled variant its configured alias in disabled_variant, and on a misconfigured default the configured default_variant_id. It no longer embeds the alias from the URL verbatim, so caller-chosen text in the log is bounded on the Access Point, the Transit Point and Fabric receive.

### Added

- **Let the operator list the only issuers external consent providers may name** — The new `external_consent_issuers` of gateway.json, set only by the operator's file, lists the issuers the credential providers of external consent may name, each compared exactly, with the origins its authorization, token and revocation endpoints and its metadata's `jwks_uri` may use beyond the issuer's own host. When it is set, a provider whose issuer is not listed, or one of whose endpoints is off the issuer's host and not listed for it, is refused with `provider_issuer_not_permitted` before the issuer's metadata is fetched, a surface cannot bind it, and its grants are neither connected, refreshed nor released. Matching its own metadata no longer suffices on a gateway whose providers another system creates. Unset, as by default, an operator's token may create a provider for any issuer whose metadata it matches, as before, but a tenant's token cannot create a provider with an issuer at all: it is refused with `external_consent_issuers_required`, which tells the operator to set the list, since the tenant would choose the endpoints its client authenticates at. An entry that is not a plain `https` issuer or origin permits nothing and is named at startup. It is read once, at startup, and a configuration reload keeps it, so provider create, surface save, the connect API and requests always agree. The issuer's RFC 9207 support is now recorded from its metadata when the provider is created.
- **Start and complete external consent connects through the connect API** — POST /api/v1/delegation-vault/connects starts a connect for a consent_mode external binding a surface serves and a principal, after checking its subject, its scopes against the binding's and its redirect URI against the provider's callback URL, and answers only the S256 challenge of a PKCE verifier the gateway keeps, encrypted, for ten minutes; a binding may have 32 open, each principal 4, counted from the connects' file names. POST /api/v1/delegation-vault/connects/{id}/complete claims the connect once, checks the authorization response's RFC 9207 iss against the provider's issuer (required when the issuer's metadata says it sends one), redeems the code at the provider's token endpoint through pinned Strict egress, ignoring any proxy as the other vendor calls of external consent do, with the verifier and resource, the client authenticating as its token_endpoint_auth_method says, a private_key_jwt client with an assertion signed for the request and addressed to the issuer, and keeps the grant only while its binding is saved with the scopes asked for. The exchange and refresh share the token request of every other credential provider type: a 30 second timeout and a 64 KiB cap. A provider created before the issuer's RFC 9207 support was recorded needs iss too. A grant it replaces, and tokens it cannot keep, are revoked. Pending connects and refresh claims are left out of storage exports, and a Transit Point with consent_mode external may not take the id target, kept for the Access Point Target's grants. Failures carry a fixed reason; the vendor's answer is never echoed or logged. The new delegation_vault.connect permission, administrators only by default, gates both, and the report of a surface's grants.
- **Report and remove grants by binding, revoking each at the vendor** — GET /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants reports a surface's external consent bindings: whether the owner is connected, how many callers are, and with principal one caller's own grant, as metadata only. DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants/{id} and DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants remove one grant or a binding's, deleting and then revoking each at its authorization server, under the surface's lock, so a grant a concurrent surface write puts back is removed too. delegation-vault is now a resource kind for these routes and the connect API, so a tenant's access token manages its own tenant's surfaces' grants, and only those. A grant asked for beyond its binding's scopes as saved now is never released, and narrowing a binding's scopes removes and revokes such grants.
- **Refresh grants by binding single-flight and retry a vendor 401 once** — A grant by binding is refreshed before it is sent once a minute or a tenth of its life is left, through a refresh claimed once across every node sharing the vault, timed when the vault's lock is held and in progress until its window ends whatever clock read its start, run on a task of its own so a dropped call never leaves it half done, asking for the scopes its connect asked for, with its provider's resource; the rotated tokens are written before the new one is used, however late they come, and a written refresh stands even when its claim cannot be cleared. invalid_grant deletes and revokes the grant, only while it is still the version refreshed, so a grant connected again meanwhile is kept; another failure, the client refused included, keeps it and retries after a doubling wait of at most 15 minutes. Refusals other than invalid_grant are counted, and once they reach gateway.json's new external_consent_refresh_refusals (3 by default) a call that cannot use the grant answers connect_required, the grant kept. A vendor's 401 whose Bearer challenge says invalid_token gets one refresh and one retry for a body up to 1 MiB; a grant that cannot be refreshed, or a refresh token refused, is removed and revoked while it still holds the token refused; a second 401 after a refresh, or one without such a challenge, keeps the grant and answers connector_unavailable. Tokens a refresh did not write are revoked, and tokens whose write cannot be confirmed are kept. A call waiting for another's refresh is woken by it. A 403 insufficient_scope answers scope_insufficient with the scope, never stepping up silently.
- **Compare granted scopes and revoke grants as each vendor needs** — An external consent provider's scope_comparison says how the scopes a token endpoint grants are compared with those asked for: exact by default, or oidc, google or microsoft, which do not take the standard identity scopes those vendors add, or Microsoft's resource-qualified and recased names, for a wider grant. revocation_ends_authorization, for a vendor such as Google whose revocation of one token ends the vendor account's whole authorization of the client, keeps the gateway from revoking a grant while another grant of the same vendor account is stored, the account being the sub of the ID token a connect obtained from the provider's issuer for its client; a grant whose account is not known is revoked. When the vault cannot tell, the removed grant is kept, encrypted and out of storage exports, for the daily sweep to decide, and revoked after 30 days all the same. Both are set at create and fixed for the provider's life. Tokens no grant keeps are audited with unkept_tokens.
- **Sweep the delegation vault's grants by binding daily** — Once a day while the node is active, expired pending connects, grants whose binding the surface no longer serves, or whose scopes it no longer permits in every place that serves it, grants written under an earlier identity hash pepper, expired grants that cannot be refreshed, and grants expired more than 30 days ago whose refresh has failed since, are removed and revoked, binding by binding, read and decided under the surface's lock. A binding whose surface or provider cannot be read is left until the next sweep. The sweep also decides the revocations removals left to it.
- **Keep revocations under way so a gateway stop does not lose them** — Before a removed grant is revoked at its vendor it is kept, encrypted like the grants, in the vault's delegated_grant_revocations directory, and forgotten once the revocation reached the vendor, whatever it answered. A gateway that stops while revocations are in flight tries them again when it starts, and the daily sweep tries any left. A revocation that could not reach its vendor is tried again the same way, at most five times in all, each attempt audited as token_revoked, ending retry:pending while it will be tried again and retry:exhausted on the last. One whose grant the vault holds again is forgotten unrevoked. Nothing is kept while at-rest encryption is off, so no token is written in plaintext.
- **Check a credential provider with an issuer against the issuer's metadata** — A credential provider can name its authorization server's `issuer`, a plain `client_id`, a `token_endpoint_auth_method` (`none`, `client_secret_basic`, `client_secret_post` or `private_key_jwt`, whose client signs its assertion with a key named by the signed-JWT settings of an `oauth2_client_credentials_jwt_auth` provider, checked at create as theirs are, and addresses it to the provider's issuer, so `jwt_audience` is refused) and an RFC 7009 `revocation_endpoint`. An external provider uses its own tenant's secrets only, checked when it is created and on every use. Such an external consent provider is checked when it is created: its endpoints must be public `https` URLs, it needs a public `https` `resource`, and the gateway reads the issuer's RFC 8414 or OpenID Connect metadata through a pinned fetch under the Strict egress posture, without requiring a `jwks_uri`, and refuses the provider with `provider_metadata_mismatch` unless its authorization, token and revocation endpoints are the ones the metadata names, the server supports its authentication method, and the metadata lists S256 PKCE. A server whose metadata lists no PKCE methods, which MCP clients refuse, is admitted only for a provider that sets `allow_unadvertised_pkce`, for a server known to enforce S256 without listing it. Its issuer, endpoints, client fields, callback URL, resource and PKCE exception never change afterwards: an update that sends another value is refused with `provider_field_immutable`, so a token that can edit providers cannot point one whose grants are stored at an endpoint of its choosing. A provider's resource must be permitted for its issuer: its host must be the issuer's host or one of its subdomains, or its origin listed for the issuer in the new `external_consent_resource_origins` of gateway.json, which only the operator sets, as exact `https` origins: an entry that is not one, such as a wildcard-looking `https://*.googleapis.com`, permits nothing and is named at startup. It is read once, at startup, and a configuration reload keeps it, so provider create, surface save and requests always agree; otherwise the provider is refused with `provider_resource_not_permitted`, a surface cannot bind it, and a grant is not released once its entry is removed. Responses name the provider's `resource_origin`. It takes no `additional_params`, `client_id_secret_ref`, consent identity strategy or API key.
- **Keep delegated grants by binding in the vault, only while encrypted at rest** — The delegation vault keeps the grants of external consent providers apart from its agent-keyed tokens, each keyed by the tenant, the surface, the Transit Point, the provider and the principal (`owner`, or a caller's identity hash), with no agent DID involved, so a grant is never released on another surface, Transit Point or tenant. A record's id is made of HMACs, under the identity hash pepper, of its provider, its binding and its whole key, so a lookup is one read and one decrypt instead of a scan of every token, the grants of one binding or provider are found by name without reading any other, and the id reveals nothing but which grants share a provider or binding. The vault refuses to store or release such a grant unless at-rest encryption is enabled with a key from the environment, a file or AWS KMS (the development `local` key source does not count) and `AG_IDENTITY_HASH_PEPPER` is set, since a pepper generated for one process would orphan every grant on restart; it warns at startup when either is missing. A fingerprint of the pepper, which reveals nothing of it, is kept beside the grants; a gateway whose pepper does not match it while grants are stored says so at startup and neither stores nor releases a grant until the pepper is restored or the grants are purged. A fingerprint or a grants directory that cannot be read at startup is named in an error and disables only grants by binding, never the agent-keyed tokens. Grants and tokens restored in plaintext are encrypted when the vault starts. A release records a grant's last use off the request path, at most once an hour. Storage exports leave these grants out. Nothing is migrated: existing agent-keyed tokens stay where they are and are never moved into grants by binding, so people connect their accounts again through external consent.
- **Release a vendor grant on a Transit Point with consent_mode external** — A Transit Point's `transit_credentials` can set `consent_mode: external` with a `subject` of `owner` (one grant every caller uses) or `caller` (one per caller's identity hash), for a credential provider with an issuer of the surface's own tenant. The Transit Point then injects the grant the vault keeps for that tenant, surface, Transit Point, provider and principal, which another system obtains through the connect API, needs no managed agent identity, never builds an authorization URL, and refuses the call before forwarding it when no live grant can be released. A call that names no caller is refused with 403, so an anonymous caller never acts as the owner. The grant goes only to the origin of the provider's `resource`: the Transit Point must target it over public `https`, never over Fabric, its id must appear in one place only, the surface's Transit Points or one variant's, so the grant is used under one Transit Point's settings, and the request is forwarded through Strict pinned egress after the destination is checked again. A save warns when an enabled variant keeps such a Transit Point from the surface while it overrides its transit settings or Access Point, since calls through the variant then use the surface's grants under the variant's own settings. Saves, including a surface's creation and tenant reassignment, are refused when such a binding has no subject, names another tenant's or an agent-keyed provider, sends the grant elsewhere, sits on a surface with `outbound_credentials`, or the vault cannot keep grants; a provider with an issuer is bound by no other consent mode, the agent-keyed legacy and `2026-07-28` paths refuse one, and surface-level `outbound_credentials` cannot ask for `external`. A surface with such a binding cannot move to another tenant until the binding is removed. The Surface Builder keeps such a binding, and its subject, when the surface is saved, and shows it read-only, offering only its removal, which revokes its grants; an external consent provider can be renamed and rescoped from the dashboard.
- **Revoke a binding's grants when a surface change removes the binding** — Deleting a surface, removing a Transit Point or its binding, changing the binding's subject or provider, moving the surface to another tenant, or disabling the only variant that holds the binding, or its transit, deletes the grants of each external consent binding the change removes while the change is saved, and revokes at the vendor what it deleted once the change is saved, at most eight at a time, each within 30 seconds, auditing each outcome; a change that fails or is refused puts them back, unrevoked, and a grant that cannot be put back is revoked. A change dropped before it reported its save, as when its client disconnects, is decided by the surface stored, read again under the surface's lock: its grants are revoked when the change was saved, and put back otherwise. Both run on a task of their own, so the change never waits on a vendor; a grant whose tokens were rotated meanwhile has its current tokens revoked. Each removed grant is kept, encrypted, until its revocation reached the vendor, so revocations in flight when the gateway stops are tried again when it starts. A change that adds a binding deletes any grant left under it, so re-adding the same surface, Transit Point id and provider, or enabling the variant again, needs a new consent. Only the grants of the bindings a change touches are read: a grant that cannot be read refuses only the changes that remove its own binding, with 503 (409 for a tenant reassignment) naming the grant, and nothing else on the gateway. A PUT or PATCH that leaves out the id of a Transit Point with external consent keeps the stored id of the one with the same alias in the same list, the surface's or the same variant's own, so a save never revokes grants for omitting ids, and a variant's copy of a Transit Point it inherits is a Transit Point of its own. Surface writes are serialised per surface, and a write removes the grants of what it changes from the surface stored at that moment, so a write built from a stale read cannot leave a grant behind. Without a secrets store a change still removes its grants, and a client that needs a secret is audited as not revoked.
- **Purge an unreadable grant by binding without reading it** — `DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants/{grant_id}` deletes one grant by binding, and `DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants` with `surface_id`, `transit_point_id`, `credential_provider_id` and an optional `tenant_id` deletes one binding's grants, without decrypting them, so a grant that cannot be read no longer blocks the removal of its binding or provider. They need `delegation_vault.delete` and refuse a tenant's token. A purged grant is not revoked at the vendor; each is audited as `token_revoked` with `vendor_revocation:skipped,reason:purged` and the operator who purged it, and a purge by binding records the binding too. While the grants stored were written under another identity hash pepper, a purge by binding answers 409 and purges nothing, since their ids are then the file names in the vault's `delegated_grants` directory.
- **Name the caller the receiving surface verified in a partner's transit token** — A transit token issued for a request a paired gateway sent over Fabric carries, as `fabric.principal_hash`, the identity hash of the caller the receiving surface's own `source_auth` or Resource Server token verified on that request, the value its Access Point would put in `user_identity_hash` for the same credential, and the claims of a verified JWT that the surface's Transit Points allowlist as `caller_context_fields`. It is set only when the surface's caller authentication tells a partner's users apart: a resource token, or a `jwt_bearer` method that names an audience (an mTLS method beside either is ignored, since it never verifies a request over Fabric). An API key, which names every key in its secret as one caller, sets none. A caller DID, presentation or payload identity the peer sends never becomes the principal; the DID a presentation from one of the connection's issuers carried is `input.transit.caller.fabric.caller_did` in policy, never verified, and a `fabric_peer` caller has no `did`. Audit records name a caller with a principal with `auth_method: fabric_verified`, its identity hash and the claims the receiving surface verified, and one without as `fabric_asserted`, each with the peer as `peer_gateway`. The principal is nested under `fabric`, so a release that does not read it sees no principal.
- **Release delegated credentials to partner callers on Transit Points that opt in** — A Transit Point that sets `delegated_credentials_for_fabric_callers: true` releases, to a call carrying a transit token issued for a partner's request, the grant of the caller in the token's `fabric.principal_hash`, and only that caller's; the caller DID the peer named never keys a grant. It is off by default, has an effect only beside `allow_fabric_callers`, and the admin API refuses it with `400` on a Transit Point that does not require the transit token. A save answers with a `Warning` header when the Transit Point has no managed identity, or when the surface's caller authentication, in the Transit Point's view, cannot tell a partner's users apart; the Transit Point then releases nothing to Fabric callers, and it checks its surface again on every call. It releases only a grant that already exists, the one the user's direct calls through the Transit Point use: consent is never started for a Fabric caller, on legacy or `2026-07-28` MCP calls, so one with no grant gets `403` and `consent_not_available_for_partners`, with no authorization URL, recorded as a `request_rejected` audit event. On a `2026-07-28` MCP call the Transit Point's resource-token caller must also be the caller in `fabric.principal_hash`. A person consents by calling the surface's Access Point directly once, with the credential the receiving surface verifies. Without the switch, or without a principal, a binding that applies refuses the call with `403` and the log gives the reason. A `PUT` that omits the switch keeps it while the Transit Point requires the transit token, since the Surface Builder does not show it.
- **⭐ Give managed agents a transit token for requests from paired gateways** — A request a paired gateway sent over Fabric reached the managed agent without a transit token, so the agent could not call its own Transit Points while serving it, and a chain of three or more organisations stopped there. A Transit Point can now set `allow_fabric_callers: true`, off by default and set through the admin API only (a Surface Builder save keeps it). The receiving gateway then issues the agent a transit token for every such request it forwards to an HTTP or HTTPS Target of that surface, opening only the Transit Points that set it, with the same key and lifetime setting as its Access Point, on buffered requests and framed streams alike. A request whose token cannot be issued is refused with `503` and recorded as a `request_rejected` audit event (`transit_token_unavailable`) rather than forwarded without it. The token names the peer gateway (its Remote gateway record and issuer DID) and the Fabric crossings the request made, and a caller DID only when a presentation from one of the connection's issuers carried it, in `fabric.caller_did`. It never sets `sub`, so a node that predates it during a rolling upgrade sees an anonymous caller. A Transit Point policy sees the peer's caller with `verification` set to `fabric_peer` and the peer in `input.transit.caller.fabric`, and the identity presentation a Transit Point signs for its agent never names the DID as the user the agent acts for. Static `target_auth` credentials are added as for any caller. `proxy://` and `a2a-proxy://` Targets never get a token, nor does a public discovery `GET` such as an agent card fetch, nor a request relayed to an MCP Target over the legacy HTTP+SSE transport, on this path or the Access Point's. Like an Access Point token, the token can be presented again until it expires; the receiving gateway logs each one it issues at `INFO` with its token ID, the peer and the hop count.
- **Refuse requests that cross Fabric more than 8 times** — Agents that call each other through their gateways could pass a request back and forth without end. Every Fabric send now sets `X-Gateway-Fabric-Hops`, the crossings the request has made, and the receiving gateway carries the count in the transit token it issues, so an agent cannot lower the count in the token it was given. A request past 8 crossings is refused with `508`, and one whose count is not an integer from 1 to 255 with `400`, before surface lookup (a framed stream resolves its surface and variant when it opens, so it is refused for one that is not available first), each recorded as a `request_rejected` audit event (`fabric_chain_too_long`, `fabric_hops_invalid`). A Transit Point to a peer gateway refuses a call that would pass the limit with `508` before forwarding it, recorded the same way at the `forward` stage. A request from a gateway that does not send the header counts as its first crossing: during a rolling upgrade, a chain through a node that predates the count starts again at 1 after it. A Transit Point reads the count from the transit token the agent sends, required or not, so a call without one starts again at 1, as do a call through a Transit Point without `allow_fabric_callers` or on a gateway with no transit token issuer, a public discovery `GET` through a Transit Point, and an agent's call to an Access Point, its own gateway's included; an agent holding an older token can present it. The limit bounds chains of Transit Point calls whose agents send the token they were given; agents calling each other's Access Points are not bounded by it, and it is not an access control.
- **Warn on save of a Fabric Transit Point that does not require the transit token** — A `fabric://` Transit Point with `require_transit_token: false` serves an agent that sends no transit token, and a call without one carries no Fabric hop count, so a chain through it can start again at 1. A create or update of a surface or one of its variants now answers with a `Warning` header naming each such Transit Point, on the surface or in a variant, so operators can turn the token back on.
- **Add access_point.public and caller_authentication.on_failure to surfaces** — `access_point.public: true` lets a surface admit callers without credentials, sets `on_failure` to `continue` on every save so a failed caller goes on to policy, and every save of such a surface answers with a `Warning` header. `caller_authentication.on_failure: reject` refuses a missing or invalid credential with `401` before policy and before the surface rate limit, as a JSON-RPC error carrying the request id for MCP callers and `application/problem+json` otherwise, on direct inbound, `proxy://`, `a2a-proxy://` and Fabric receive. The dashboard adds a Public access switch to the Access Point panel, and shows a surface stored without `public` as a legacy Access Point that it can close. A save of a legacy surface that authenticates no caller answers with a `Warning` header saying it admits callers without credentials.
- **Report whether an Access Point is ready for external access** — Surface responses from the admin API carry a read-only top-level `access_point_status`, `ready` or `not_ready`, for the surface the Access Point URL serves: its default variant when it has one, otherwise the base. Variant responses report it for that variant. It is never stored, and a write that sends it back has it dropped. The dashboard shows "Not ready for external access" under the Access Point URL, with a Configure Authentication action, until the saved surface is ready. Each request a not-ready Access Point refuses is recorded in the Audit Log as `request_rejected` with the reason `access_point_not_ready`.
- **⭐ Set who can call a Transit Point from the Surface Builder** — The Transit Point panel has a **Who can call this Transit Point** section: the transit token switch, a **Caller authentication** choice (an API key from the secret store or the provider, or a JWT bearer with its strategy, audiences, token header and scheme) that sets the Transit Point's `source_auth`, and an **Open to any caller** switch behind a confirmation. The panel applies the gateway's save rules first: a Transit Point that authenticates no caller and is not open, one that is open beside the token or a method, and a method missing its secret, header, strategy or audience cannot be saved, and the panel says why; an empty token scheme is allowed, as the gateway accepts it. A refusal from the gateway shows in plain words in the same section, of the base or the variant it names. Choosing the API key provider warns that it accepts the same keys as an Access Point using the provider, and a save points it at the surface being saved, so one copied from a template accepts that surface's keys. A `source_auth` set through the API with mTLS is shown and kept.
- **Show the gateway's save warnings on the Transit Point they name** — A save answered with `Warning` headers, such as an open Transit Point reachable from other hosts, a JWT that accepts the Access Point's own tokens, or a strategy that shares an issuer, now shows each one on its Transit Point and in a toast, and keeps it until that Transit Point's caller authentication, open marking or listener changes. The shared-audience and shared-issuer warnings now name the variant that defines the Transit Point, as the exposure warning does, so each lands on the right Transit Point. A create's warnings are shown once on the new surface's page, not again on a refresh, and a save's warnings never show on another surface. Before saving, the panel warns when an open Transit Point's listener URL is one other hosts reach.
- **⭐ Let a Transit Point authenticate its callers with an API key, JWT or mTLS** — A caller that cannot carry a transit token, such as a cloud-hosted agent, had no way to prove who it was to a Transit Point, so operators turned the token off and served anyone who reached it. A Transit Point now takes its own `source_auth`, checked by the same source authentication as an Access Point before anything is forwarded: `api_key` or `api_key_provider` read from a header, `jwt_bearer`, or `mtls` from the outbound listener's TLS handshake or a trusted proxy; an inbound listener that passes `/outbound` requests on now drops the forwarded-client-cert header, so no certificate reaches a Transit Point that way unverified. A missing or refused credential is refused with `401` that says only that it is missing or invalid, and recorded as an `auth_failed` audit event, and the credential is never sent to the target. The admitted caller is named on the Transit Point's policy and delegation audit records. The verified caller reaches the Transit Point's policy input as `input.transit.client` with `verification: source_auth`, apart from the end user a transit token names in `input.transit.caller`, so a policy can allow only named callers, and keys per-caller delegated credentials on legacy MCP, A2A and HTTP requests by the same identity hash the Access Point uses. The secrets, strategies, API-key parents and certificates it names are checked on save as an Access Point's are, so a tenant access token can name only its own or appliance-wide ones. A `jwt_bearer` must name an audience, and a save answers with a `Warning` header when it accepts the Access Point's own tokens (the same strategy and a shared audience, such as the resource an MCP Access Point's protected resource metadata names for the base surface or a variant), or when its strategy names the same issuer as another strategy the surface accepts, as callers verified through either share delegated credentials. A `PUT` of the surface or of one variant that omits a Transit Point's `source_auth`, as a dashboard save does, keeps it; `"source_auth": null` removes it, and a Transit Point that is then left authenticating no caller is refused with `400`.
- **Warn when an open Transit Point is reachable from other hosts** — A Transit Point that serves callers it does not authenticate gives anyone who reaches it its target credentials. When other hosts can reach one, because its outbound listener, or an inbound listener that shares a host name with it and passes `/outbound` requests on, binds an address other than loopback or has an external URL that is not this host's own (private addresses included), the admin API answers the save of its surface with a `Warning` header naming it and those addresses, and the outbound listener logs it at `WARN`. Every open Transit Point is logged at `WARN` when it is mounted. The dashboard does not show the header.
- **Let a Transit Point policy allow or deny a call by its verified caller** — A Transit Point policy could not tell its callers apart, because the caller the transit token carries reached the audit record but not the policy input. Outbound policy input now has `input.transit.caller`, the end user the transit token names: whether the caller is `verified`, how (`transit_token`, `unverified` or `anonymous`), the caller's `user_identity_hash`, the DID the token asserts, and the caller context fields that Transit Point allowlists. MCP tool gating conditions on a Transit Point read the same input. A call without a valid transit token says it is anonymous. A Transit Point that does not require a transit token still verifies one the agent sends, so the caller it names reaches policy; a token there that does not verify is ignored, not refused. Only policy reads that token: such a Transit Point still delegates no credential, binds no `caller_source: transit_token` field and does not end a stream when the token expires. A client the Transit Point verified itself, by its resource-server token, is in `input.transit.client` with its method, `user_identity_hash` and subject, so a policy can tell the end user and the client apart when a call carries both. A Transit Point's response policy gets the same `input.transit`, and `input.source_auth` for a client the Transit Point verified, so it can decide who receives a response. Inbound response policies have no `input.transit`, so a response policy shared with the inbound legs must not deny or allow on `not input.transit.caller.verified`. A policy that allows only one caller should test `verified` first, as a token that only names a DID is reported as unverified.
- **Set how long a surface's transit tokens live, up to an operator ceiling** — Transit tokens always lived 300 seconds, so a managed agent's turn that ran longer, such as a long tool call, lost its Transit Points part way. A surface's `transit.transit_token_lifetime_secs` now sets how long the tokens its Access Point issues live, from 30 seconds to the appliance's longest lifetime; unset means 300, or the longest when that is below 300. The longest is `transit_token_max_lifetime_secs` in `gateway.json`: 300 seconds unless set, from 30 to 900. It is read once, when the gateway starts: anything but a whole number from 30 to 900 stops it from starting, a change takes a restart, and a reload keeps the startup value, never fails on it, and logs a warning while `gateway.json` holds another value. The Access Point issues tokens for a stored lifetime above the longest, or below 30, held to the bound, with a warning per surface at startup and on reload; neither stops the gateway. A Transit Point checks a token, after checking it was issued for that surface, against the lifetime its surface sets when the token is presented, held to 30 to 900 seconds and independent of any instance's settings, so instances accept each other's tokens; shortening the lifetime refuses the tokens already issued for longer while it is in force, and lengthening it never extends a token already issued. Shortening is not revocation: rotate the transit token key to revoke issued tokens. A `PUT` that omits the lifetime, as a dashboard save does, keeps the stored value, also when it omits a stored base `transit` block with no Transit Points, and a merge `PATCH` with `null` resets it. The admin API refuses a new lifetime outside the bounds, naming the setting, or one set in a variant, but accepts a save that keeps the stored value.
- **Add JWT Bearer and Client Credentials JWT credential provider types** — Credential providers gain oauth2_jwt_bearer, which signs an RS256 or ES256 JWT as the grant (RFC 7523 section 2.1, for example a Google service account for Vertex AI Agent Engine), and oauth2_client_credentials_jwt_auth, which authenticates the client with a signed JWT instead of a client secret (private_key_jwt, for example an Entra certificate credential or an Ory client with a JWKS). The private key and the optional certificate, whose thumbprint Entra uses to find the key, are secret references, checked to be PEM when the provider is saved.
- **Let target authentication use a credential provider** — A Target or Transit Point target_auth can use method credential_provider to send a client credentials, JWT Bearer, Client Credentials JWT or API key provider's token, Authorization Bearer by default. Issued tokens are cached in the delegation vault per surface and renewed before they expire. The dashboard offers it on the Managed Agent and Transit Point, and a surface save refuses an authorization-code provider.
- **Warn in the dashboard when an Entra v2.0 provider sets resource** — The Microsoft identity platform v2.0 token endpoint rejects the resource parameter (AADSTS901002), so the credential provider form and list now warn when a provider with such an endpoint sets one, and say to put the audience in the scopes as /.default.
- **⭐ Name the surface variant on every audit record and log line of a request** — Every audit record written while serving a surface request carries surface_variant: the variant's alias, _base_ when the base surface served it, or _unknown_ when the URL named a variant the surface doesn't have. Policy decisions, refusals, delegation, VP, identity binding, trust check and payment records of a request all carry it, so audits and integrations can tell which variant a request ran on. The policy_audit, identity_binding_audit, trust_check_audit and credential_delegation log lines carry the same field, and the request spans carry surface.variant. Records outside surface requests, and records written before the upgrade, have none.
- **Offer AUDIT_SURFACE_VARIANT to audit integration templates** — Audit integration templates can use AUDIT_SURFACE_VARIANT, the record's surface variant, empty outside surface requests and when the surface's default variant is misconfigured.
- **Show the surface variant and refusal details on the Audit Log** — The Audit Log shows which variant a request record ran on and says it in the record's description, reads a gateway-wide policy decision as applying to every surface, and describes request refusals with their stage, reason, status and the variant asked for. Policy change records show the slot's variant, or the base surface.

### Changed

- **Refuse a Transit Point call whose grant is missing with a fixed reason** — A Transit Point call whose consent_mode external grant cannot be released is refused with a fixed reason instead of a generic 503: connect_required (403, with the subject and, for a caller, a hint of the caller), scope_insufficient (403) or connector_unavailable (503), as a JSON-RPC error with the reason in its data on MCP Transit Points and as a problem JSON on the others. A 2026-07-28 MCP request to such a Transit Point is refused as an unsupported protocol version.
- **Ask an MCP Transit Point's target for an uncompressed answer** — An MCP Transit Point forwards Accept-Encoding identity in place of the managed agent's value, since the gateway reads the answer as sent for tool gating and response checks, and refuses with 502 an answer compressed all the same.
- **Revoke readable grants when an operator removes grants by binding** — DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants/{id} and DELETE /api/v1/delegation-vault/grants now delete and revoke every grant they can read, and purge, unrevoked, only those that cannot be decrypted or parsed, which an operator alone may do: a session, or an access token with neither a tenant nor a resource pattern. A grant that cannot be read during a disk or key service outage is neither removed nor purged, and the removal answers 503. The binding query takes hop (transit_point_id is still accepted), and credential_provider_id may be left out while the surface binds one there. It answers removed, grant_ids and purged. Each removal deletes only the connection it read, refreshed since or not, never a grant the person connected again in between.
- **Make credential provider slugs unique per tenant** — A credential provider's `provider_id` is now unique per tenant, and among the untenanted providers. The duplicate check now compares the slug as it is stored, trimmed and in lower case, so `GitHub` and `github` conflict, and a create whose duplicate check fails is refused instead of proceeding. A provider cannot move into a tenant that already has a provider with its slug (409).
- **Admit a partner's verified caller by a Transit Point's identity-hash rules** — A Transit Point policy sees a Fabric caller with a `fabric.principal_hash` as `verified`, with that hash as `input.transit.caller.user_identity_hash`, so a rule that admits a verified caller by its identity hash also admits that caller when a partner's request for it arrives over Fabric and the agent calls the Transit Point with the token issued for it. Add `not input.transit.caller.fabric` to keep partner requests out on a Transit Point that requires the transit token.
- **Serve Transit Point calls from agents handling requests from partner gateways** — A Transit Point that requires a transit token answered `401` to a managed agent serving a request from a paired gateway, because the agent had no token to send. One that sets `allow_fabric_callers` now serves that agent with the token its gateway issued for the request. The agent receives only that token: an `X-Transit-Token` the peer gateway sent is removed on receive. No `X-Gateway-Fabric-Hops` a caller, agent or peer sends is forwarded, to an HTTP target or over Fabric.
- **Turn a Transit Point's transit token back on for agents serving partners** — Operators who set `require_transit_token: false` on a Transit Point, with `allow_unauthenticated_callers: true`, only so that agents serving requests from paired gateways could call it, should set it back to `true`, set `allow_fabric_callers: true` and remove `allow_unauthenticated_callers`: those agents then get a transit token. A Transit Point that does not require the token serves anyone who reaches it, and an agent that sends no token starts the Fabric hop count again.
- **Show a surface save and its warnings in one warning toast** — The surface create and edit pages show a save that drew warnings as one toast, the saved message followed by "with warnings:" and each warning, with warning styling instead of error styling. The gateway's `Warning` headers come first, then the dashboard's own.
- **Send a delegated payment only the headers an Access Point forwards** — A surface that delegates its payment (`agent_pay`) now sends the payment gateway the caller's headers that the Access Point forwards to its own Target, and `Authorization` only when it carries a `Payment` credential that neither the surface's `source_auth` nor its Resource Server reads. Other `Authorization` values, `Proxy-Authorization` and other hop-by-hop headers, caller-info headers, mapped headers the surface strips and `X-Transit-Token` stay on this gateway.
- **Say when Connection Point Connected is raised in the integration catalog** — The catalog described connection_point.connected as someone connecting through a connection point with its secret. It now says it is raised when another gateway pairs through a connection point's invitation with its secret, which is the only time it is sent; a failed attempt sends nothing. See docs/INTEGRATIONS.md.
- **Deprecate sign_requests, which has no effect, and warn of a stored false** — `transit.sign_requests` ships in generated configs but the gateway never read it. It is documented as having no effect and deprecated: a Transit Point's VP follows its `managed_identity` and `identity_injection.inject_vp`. It is never refused, and a stored `false` logs a warning at load, since it may be read as meaning no VP is sent. The built-in surface templates no longer set it.
- **Turn log redaction on by default for every configuration source** — logging.redaction in gateway.json now applies a built-in rule set for credentials, private keys, personal access tokens and email addresses unless it sets enabled to false, and rules written by the operator replace the built-in set. It is read from gateway.json for every configuration source, including DynamoDB, where it was never loaded. Span and log attributes named like a credential, such as authorization, cookie, token or session_id, become <redacted>, and the built-in rules also match JSON-quoted values such as "authorization": "Bearer ...". With the built-in rules the access log's mcp_sid field is masked in full. See docs/CONFIGURATION_REFERENCE.md.
- **Name the field and rule, not the value, in extension validation errors** — Identity extension, rules engine and schema validation errors, which are returned to callers, logged and shown on the dashboard, no longer quote the rejected value or the payload. They name the field path, the rule and its configured constraint, and the JSON type for a type mismatch.
- **Rebuild a trust registry from its stored keys when it is reconnected** — Reconnect no longer falls back to accepting the registry's OOB invitation again once the registry has an identity. That fallback created a new identity over the stored keys, which the registry admin rejects, and for `did:peer` and `did:webvh` registries it could not be undone. A failed reconnect now returns 502, logs the cause instead of returning it, and leaves the stored status and identity unchanged. A registry whose setup never connected has no identity yet, so reconnecting it still accepts its OOB invitation again and completes the setup, unless a connection for it is already installed. The new identity is saved onto the registry's current record even when the request is dropped during the build, so edits made meanwhile are kept. A successful reconnect moves a Failed registry to Connecting, where it waits for the registry to approve it, and keeps any other status, so a registry is no longer shown as Connected before the registry approves it. A registry marked Failed before this change by a dropped connection stays Connecting after it is reconnected, because reconnecting does not ask the registry to approve the gateway again, so delete it and add it again.
- **Refuse to create or reconnect trust registries on a Standby node** — Create and reconnect now return 503 on a Standby node before anything is written, and queries there fail without opening a connection. A Standby node could open sessions under the Active node's DIDs, dropping its sessions, and write Failed into the shared store.
- **Check JWT key material on the credential provider validate endpoint** — POST /api/v1/credential-providers/validate now requires the credential provider edit permission, applies the caller's tenant to the secrets it names, and for a JWT Bearer or Client Credentials JWT provider checks that the signing key and certificate secrets hold PEM key material before it tests the token endpoint.

### Fixed

- **Document the direct vendor calls and limits of external consent** — The deployment requirements now name the code exchange and token refresh among the external consent calls that ignore a proxy, so an operator on a proxy-only network opens direct egress to each provider's token endpoint host. The credential delegation guide now says that a variant listing no Transit Points of its own releases the surface's grants under its own transit settings and Access Point, with a Warning on save; that a provider delete answers 503 while the surface store is unavailable; and that surface writes of every tenant wait while a deleted provider's grants are read and deleted.
- **Keep retrying a revocation the sweep decides while its vendor is unreachable** — A revocation left to the daily sweep was forgotten once the sweep tried it, whether or not it reached the vendor, so an outage left the tokens valid with nothing left to retry. It is now kept as a pending revocation, retried up to five times like any other.
- **Answer 503 when removing a binding's grants while its surface cannot be read** — Removing a binding's grants treated a surface store error as a deleted surface, and could answer 200 having removed nothing, or a misleading 400. It now answers 503 surfaces_unavailable and removes nothing.
- **Refresh a narrowed grant only for the scopes it holds** — A refresh asked again for every scope the connect asked for, even those the person did not grant, which RFC 6749 forbids and standards-following servers refuse. It now asks for the scopes the grant still holds, compared by the provider's scope_comparison, and an answer that names no scope keeps the grant's scopes rather than recording the requested ones.
- **Retry pending revocations when a standby node becomes active** — Revocations kept when a gateway stopped were retried at startup only on an active node, so a node that started in standby left them until its daily sweep. They are now retried each time the node becomes active.
- **Leave a connect open when its completion is refused for iss or its provider** — Completing a connect claimed it, wiping its verifier, before iss and the provider were checked, so a missing iss or a provider refused for a moment forced a new consent. Those checks now run first, and a completion they refuse leaves the connect open.
- **Give a variant's Transit Points an id when a save leaves it out** — A surface save gave an id only to the surface's own Transit Points, so a variant's Transit Point sent without one was stored with an empty id. A variant's Transit Points now get an id, and their aliases are checked within the variant's list, as the surface's are.
- **Stop logging that the delegation vault is encrypted when it is not** — The delegation vault's startup line said it was encrypted whatever the configuration. It now says whether it is encrypted at rest, and warns when it is not that delegated tokens are written in plaintext.
- **⭐ Deliver connection point created, updated and deleted events to integrations** — Creating, editing or deleting a connection point through the management API only logged that its integration trigger was not implemented, and the integration catalog offered no such events, so no email, Slack, webhook or stream integration could be told about them. The catalog now lists connection_point.created, connection_point.updated and connection_point.deleted, and each is delivered to every active integration whose event set holds it, like the gateway events. updated is raised when a save changes the name, description, enabled state or exposed surfaces, and a save that changes none of them raises nothing. They carry the connection point's id, name, description, DID, gateway and mediator, with OLD_STATE and NEW_STATE, never its invitation or secret. Connection points the gateway creates or removes itself, such as those of a pairing or an expired invitation, raise none of them, and these events are separate from the event hook calls of the same names. Add the events to an event set to receive them; upgrading does not add them to existing event sets. See docs/INTEGRATIONS.md.
- **Stack the Audit Log list above its details on smaller laptop screens** — Below 1440px wide the Audit Log now shows the event list above the event details and the page scrolls as one, instead of two cramped side-by-side panels with short scroll boxes; selecting an event scrolls to its details, and the search, filters, Forward records and Refresh stay pinned at the top while the page scrolls. On wider screens the two panels fill the page height without the page scrolling past the footer, and the event details use the full height next to the About banner.
- **Keep long tags and owner names from stretching list tables** — Long tags on surfaces, secrets and certificates, and long owner names on MCP proxies, now wrap inside their table cell instead of widening the column. The lists no longer scroll sideways on laptop screens, the Name and Description columns keep their width, and every row action stays on screen.
- **Fit Identities, Connection Points, Credential Audit, Metrics, Tasks on laptops** — The Identities table no longer scrolls sideways at 1280px wide, so every row action stays reachable. Connection Points show their four actions in a compact block, and Credential Audit gives its Downstream Provider and Agent Surface columns room, so names no longer wrap into tall rows. A long identity in the Metrics flow chart is cut to 22 characters plus "..." like the source and target labels, so it no longer runs over its neighbours; hover shows the full identity. On Tasks the tag filter sits under the title and Refresh stays at the right end of the header.
- **Stop the Surface Builder offering reference transit tokens and request signing** — The Transit Settings panel offered a "Reference (server-side store)" token mode that every save then refused, a "Sign every outbound request" switch that has no effect, and an `unsigned` canvas summary, and a variant's overrides carried both settings. The panel now shows the embedded mode without a choice, and every save sends `transit_token_mode: embedded`, Neither the panel, the Transit Point defaults nor a variant's overrides set `sign_requests`, and the gateway writes it back only when it is `false`.
- **Refuse a surface template that asks for reference transit tokens** — A surface template whose surface set `transit_token_mode: reference`, on the surface or in a variant, was saved, created or imported without a check, and every surface created from it was then refused. Template create, update and import now refuse it with the same 400 as a surface save.
- **Store DID Auth sessions apart from dashboard sessions** — DID Auth sessions are now kept in the didauth subdirectory of storage_paths.sessions, and each start moves DID Auth session files left in the sessions directory there. Both session stores used to read the same directory, so every start logged an error for each file of the other kind, naming the session token in the file path. See docs/SOURCE_AUTH.md.
- **Send the service resource with OTLP logs and flush them on shutdown and reload** — Log records exported over OTLP now carry service.name, version and environment, and pending records are flushed when the gateway stops or the OpenTelemetry settings are saved.
- **Build each trust registry's connection once, however many queries need it** — Trust Checks, Trust Recorders, issuer registration, startup and reconnect could each build a trust registry client at the same time, and the mediator's single websocket per DID made them drop each other's sessions. The gateway now runs one build per registry, which every caller joins and waits for within its own timeout. A build that outlives its caller is still installed for the next query instead of being left half-built, and a build aborted after 90 seconds shuts down the client it created so it cannot hold the registry's websocket. Public registries share one temporary connection per DID, and a stored registry is never queried under a throwaway identity: a query fails when the trust registry store cannot be read.
- **Retry a trust registry in the background after its connection fails** — A dropped or failed connection to a connected trust registry no longer marks it Failed. A build that fails is retried in the background by the bootstrap reconnect_policy (by default 30 seconds after the first failure, doubling up to every 30 minutes) until it connects, is deleted or the node goes to Standby; a websocket that drops on a working connection is reconnected by the DIDComm client. Reconnect starts a build at once and restarts the backoff. Failed is now set only when the registry rejects the gateway or the first OOB acceptance fails, and a problem report answering a query that already timed out no longer marks a registry Failed. Failed registries are neither retried nor queried automatically, so a registry marked Failed before this change by a dropped connection fails its Trust Checks, Trust Recorders and issuer registration until it is deleted and added again. A build that cannot read the trust registry store or the registry's keys is retried too.
- **Keep a deleted trust registry deleted** — A connection still being built, a status change from the registry or an update could write a deleted trust registry back to storage or open a connection for it. Deleting a registry now stops its builds and retries and fails the queries waiting on it, waiting up to 90 seconds for a build still running, and every write except create skips a registry that no longer exists. An update of a registry deleted at the same time returns 404. A delete whose record cannot be removed returns 500 and keeps the registry and its keys usable, and a delete finishes even when its request is dropped while it waits.
- **Fetch an agent card protected by an expiring OAuth token** — The gateway's agent card fetch can now present a credential provider's token, so agents whose card sits behind an Entra or Google OAuth token, such as Foundry and Vertex AI Agent Engine agents, publish their card through the gateway.
- **Renew delegated service tokens before they expire** — Client-credentials delegation tokens are renewed 60 seconds before they expire, and a token response without expires_in is refused instead of being cached forever. A failed token request now logs the token endpoint's error instead of returning it in the delegation audit detail.
- **Keep the surface variant on metrics of a stream the client aborts** — A streamed response whose client disconnects before its end now records its connection metric with the request's surface variant and id, as a stream that completes does. The stream was dropped outside its request, so the metric carried no variant.
- **Reject a default_variant_id that names no variant on a surface without variants** — Saving a surface whose default_variant_id names no variant is refused with 400 whether or not the surface has variants; a surface with no variants used to save with one, and its base surface served its requests. The gateway drops such a stored default at startup, with a warning naming the surface and the dropped id, so the base surface keeps serving them instead of every path refusing them with 500 as misconfigured_default.
- **Refresh the enabled variants of a surface whose default variant is disabled** — Saving a surface whose default variant is disabled now refreshes the Targets and overrides the Access Point serves its enabled variants with. They used to keep the ones from before the save, as the whole cache entry was left as it was; requests with no alias are still refused as the disabled default.
- **Ask a Fabric peer for the pinned variant on the MPP auto-pay retry too** — When a peer gateway answers a ForwardRequest with 402 and the Access Point's MPP auto-pay pays it, the paid retry now carries the alias the fabric:// Target pins, as the first request does. It carried none, so the peer could serve the paid request with its default variant or base surface instead of the pinned one.

### Removed

- **Remove the DEBUG and CONFIG-LOAD lines printed at startup** — Startup no longer prints DEBUG, [CONFIG-LOAD] and [V8] lines to standard output and standard error, which bypassed log levels and redaction.
- **Drop variant_alias from request_completed audit records** — The request_completed audit record no longer declares a variant_alias field. No release ever wrote it; the record's variant is surface_variant, as on every other request record.

## [1.0.3] - 2026-10-29

### Breaking

- **Refuse transit tokens issued before the upgrade or by an unshared instance** — Transit tokens issued before the upgrade are refused once, so a managed agent's Transit Point calls fail until its next inbound request brings a new token, at most five minutes later.
